Should something impure come upon a man, let him cover it with his garment, wipe it away with the underside of his sandal, or bury it so that it cannot be seen and will harm no one, and in that there is nothing wrong.

From Ali it is related that he said: "Do not make the mosques a place for idle talk." And he told of a man from the people of Yemen who entered the mosque of the Messenger of Allah having eaten garlic, so that its odor filled the air. The Messenger of Allah was troubled by it and said: "Whoever eats this plant, let him not draw near the mosque." He taught that upon entering the mosque one should say: بِسْمِ اللَّهِ وَبِاللَّهِ، السَّلَامُ عَلَيْكَ أَيُّهَا النَّبِيُّ وَرَحْمَةُ اللَّهِ وَبَرَكَاتُهُ، السَّلَامُ عَلَيْنَا وَعَلَى عِبَادِ اللَّهِ الصَّالِحِينَ, and then greet the people of the mosque. He said: "It is among the rights of the mosque that when you enter it, you pray two rakahs therein," meaning during those times in which voluntary prayers are permitted, which we have already set forth.

Ali said that for one who does not know the precise direction, the qibla lies between the east and the west, and that the qibla faces the Sacred House. Allah, mighty and majestic, said: ﴿فَوَلِّ وَجْهَكَ شَطْرَ الْمَسْجِدِ الْحَرَامِ وَحَيْثُ مَا كُنْتُمْ فَوَلُّوا وُجُوهَكُمْ شَطْرَهُ﴾ [البقرة: ١٤٤].

The Messenger of Allah said that one should not pray except in clean, dry clothing, and that to pray without a barrier before oneself is a mark of coarseness. He said: "If one of you prays in an open field, let him set before him something like the back of a saddle, if he can find it." From Ali it is related that he disliked a man's praying toward a sleeping person placed before him, or toward a woman's shoes, unless he first sets a barrier before him. He said: "If you pray toward a barrier, draw near to it, and let the space between you and it be the measure of your prostration when you prostrate." For the Messenger of Allah said: "When one of you prays, let him draw near to the barrier and not leave a gap between himself and the qibla, lest the devil take it as a passage." He forbade the display of images in the place of prayer. And he said: as for the mosque, or the room in a man's house where he prays, if he does not turn to face the people but wishes only to make room and put the space to use, there is nothing wrong in that.

As for the imamate, the Messenger of Allah said: "Set forward the best among you in your prayer, for the imam stands as the delegate of the people." From Ali it is related that he said the sick man should not lead the sound in prayer, this being particular to the Messenger of Allah alone, and that one who is bound should not lead those who are free. We have narrated from the Ahl al-Bayt that they disliked the imamate of the leper, the one wasted by leprosy, the child of fornication, the insane, the enslaved, and the blind, unless such a one leads rightly when there is need of him, and he is not to be taken as a settled imam. Likewise they did not hold that the bedouin of full standing, nor the one who has undergone a prescribed punishment, nor the madman, should lead, though they permitted that such men lead one another, each leading those in a condition like his own. They did not hold prayer permissible behind those who bear enmity toward the family of Muhammad and openly declare their hatred of them, except in a state of dissimulation, and we seek refuge in Allah from such affliction. Whoever is tried with this prays behind one whose condition is as described only if he fears for himself. He forms the intention for himself alone and does not follow the man as imam. He bows when the man bows, prostrates when he prostrates, and departs when he departs, intending all of it for himself as though he prayed alone with no imam.

If one who has performed tayammum is more deserving of the imamate, he may lead those who have performed ablution. And whoever leads a people in prayer, then afterward learns that he was not in a state of purity, must repeat the prayer, and so must they. Umar once led the people, then, when he had finished, said: "O people, Umar has led you while he was in a state of major impurity." They asked, "What then do you hold?" He said: "In my view, I must repeat, but there is no repetition upon you." Yet Ali said: "Rather, the repetition is upon you and upon them, for the people bow and prostrate by their imam."

The most deserving of the imamate is the most excellent, the best in recitation, the most learned. If they are equal in that, then the eldest in years takes precedence. When a man leads two others, he stands before them and they form a row behind him. When he leads one, he sets him at his right; and if another comes and joins the prayer, the newcomer advances and stands behind the imam. There is no harm in the imam's leading with a light barrier between himself and the man behind him. They disliked the imam's lengthening the prayer when leading a congregation, for among them may be the weak and the one with a pressing need, while one who prays alone does better to lengthen it. They said a woman leads other women but does not stand ahead of them; rather she stands in the midst of them, in a single row with them.

If the imam errs or slips, passing from one surah to another, or leaving out something of the Quran, or moving to something else of it, there is no harm in that, and those behind him are not obliged to correct him so long as he continues to recite. But if he falters, halting and falling silent, they prompt him and open for him from behind what he stumbled over, and once he continues they fall silent again. Even if two men stand in prayer, each intending to lead the other, and their prayers are completed and they learn of this only afterward, their prayers are valid, for the imam's prayer is not spoiled if no one stands behind him or if all turn away. But if each had intended to follow the other, their prayers are void, for each held himself to be a follower praying behind an imam when there was no imam over him.

They disliked the imam's offering voluntary prayer, after the obligatory one, in the very spot where he led, and preferred that he move to another place. They recommended that, when he turns to depart, he turn to his right, and that he not break through the rows or walk between them, and that he sit a while in his place after the salutation, calling upon Allah so that whoever missed part of the prayer may complete what he missed.

Concerning the congregation and the rows, the Messenger of Allah said: "My community will not cease to be shielded from affliction until they deal in usury, bribery appears among them, they sever the ties of kinship, they abandon prayer in congregation, and they no longer make for this House. When they do that, they will be left without regard." And he said that whoever prays the five prayers in congregation is to be thought well of in every good and his testimony accepted, so long as no ill is known of him. Abu Jafar ibn Ali was asked whether prayer in congregation is obligatory. He said: "The prayer is obligatory, but the congregation is not obligatory in every prayer; it is Sunnah. Yet whoever forsakes it out of aversion, turning from it and from the community of the believers with neither excuse nor cause, in disdain of it, has no prayer."

It is related of Ali that he once stood the whole night in prayer, and when dawn came he slept and missed the Fajr in the mosque. The Messenger of Allah, not seeing him, came to the house of Fatimah after finishing the prayer and asked: "Daughter, what has kept your cousin from witnessing the prayer with us?" She told him what had happened, and he said: "What he missed of the Fajr in congregation is better than a whole night's standing." Then he roused him and said as much to him. And he said: "O Ali, the earth cries out to Allah from the sleeper upon it at the rising of the sun." Ali said: "That I should pray the Isha and the Fajr in congregation is dearer to me than that I should spend all that lies between them in worship." And he said: "Beneath the shade of the Throne, on a Day when there is no shade but its shade, is a man who goes out from his house, having perfected his purification, walking to one of the houses of Allah to fulfill therein one of the obligations of Allah, and who perishes upon that path."

The Messenger of Allah said: "The best of the rows in prayer is the first, and the best of the rows in funeral prayer is the last, and the best of the rows for women is the rearmost." Ali said the best place in the first row is at the imam's right, and Jafar ibn Muhammad said the best of it is what lies nearest the imam. We have narrated from the Ahl al-Bayt that they commanded the completing of the rows and the closing of the gaps. Whoever stands in a row and sees a gap in the row before him may walk forward to fill it, and whoever feels crowded in the row may step back to a gap in the row behind him and stand in it. There is no harm in one man's praying alone behind the row when he finds no place within it, and they permitted one who finds no place in the first row to advance and stand level with the imam when he can do no other. The least space between two rows is the measure by which the worshipper may fall into prostration, prostrate, and rise from it, for when the people of a row cannot reach the place of prostration there is no prayer for them. They permitted forming rows between the pillars when nothing else can be found. And the Messenger of Allah said: "Straighten your rows, align your shoulders, and do not differ, lest your hearts differ and the devil slip in among you as the little ones of the hadhaf slip among the flock." The hadhaf are small black sheep, and he likened their entering among the large sheep to the devil's entering between the rows when he finds a breach in them, to corrupt the prayer of its people and to occupy their hearts against it. We seek refuge in Allah from the devil and his works.

As for the rows of prayer and their foundation, the Messenger of Allah said: "Deeds are only by intentions, and to each man belongs only what he intended." Ali said: "Whoever prays with his intention set upon the prayer alone, nothing else entering into it, has it accepted from him when it is sound in what is outward and what is inward." Jafar ibn Muhammad said it does not befit the worshipper to enter into prayer until he has formed the intention, settled his heart upon it, and determined which prayer it is, whether an obligation, a Sunnah, or a voluntary act; and if it is an obligation, that he intend which of the five it is, holding this firmly as he enters. He opens the prayer with the takbir, raising his hands with palms open and fingers together and extended, his fingertips level with his ears, then letting his hands fall along his sides and thighs. He does not lay one hand upon the other, nor hold them beneath his breast as some of the common people do, claiming this to be from the words of Allah, mighty and majestic: ﴿فَصَلِّ لِرَبِّكَ وَانْحَرْ﴾ [الكوثر: ٢]. Abu Jafar Muhammad ibn Ali was asked about this and said: "The sacrificing is after the prayer of the Eid, and the sacrifice within the prayer is uprightness in standing, that the worshipper hold his spine and his chest erect." He said the Messenger of Allah forbade laying the hands one upon the other in prayer, saying that this is what the Jews do, and he commanded that the hands be let free. And Abu Jafar Muhammad ibn Ali said that a woman, when she stands in prayer, places her hands upon her breast to cover it.

The worshipper then turns himself and says: وَجَّهْتُ وَجْهِيَ لِلَّهِ نَاظِرِ السَّمَوَاتِ وَالْأَرْضِ حَنِيفًا مُسْلِمًا وَمَا أَنَا مِنَ الْمُشْرِكِينَ، إِنَّ صَلَاتِي وَنُسُكِي وَمَحْيَايَ وَمَمَاتِي لِلَّهِ رَبِّ الْعَالَمِينَ لَا شَرِيكَ لَهُ، وَبِذَلِكَ أُمِرْتُ وَأَنَا مِنَ الْمُسْلِمِينَ، عَلَى مِلَّةِ إِبْرَاهِيمَ وَدِينِ مُحَمَّدٍ وَوِلَايَةِ عَلِيِّ بْنِ أَبِي طَالِبٍ أَمِيرِ الْمُؤْمِنِينَ، وَأَبْرَأُ إِلَى اللَّهِ مِنْ أَعْدَائِهِ الظَّالِمِينَ، أَعُوذُ بِاللَّهِ السَّمِيعِ الْعَلِيمِ مِنَ النَّارِ وَمِنَ الشَّيْطَانِ الرَّجِيمِ. He utters this with his tongue, quietly and with reverence, not aloud where the recitation is silent nor where it is aloud.

Then he opens the recitation with بِسْمِ ٱللَّهِ ٱلرَّحْمَنِ ٱلرَّحِيمِ, saying it quietly where the recitation is quiet and aloud where it is aloud, and he must not do otherwise, for it is a verse of the Book of Allah, mighty and majestic. Some have claimed it is not of the Book of Allah, yet it stands established at the head of every surah, revealed thus by Jibril to the Messenger of Allah; and the Ahl al-Bayt, together with many of the generality, agreed upon this and narrated that it was so revealed, and that the completion of one surah and the beginning of the next were known when بِسْمِ ٱللَّهِ ٱلرَّحْمَنِ ٱلرَّحِيمِ came down. Jafar ibn Muhammad said: "Dissimulation is my religion and the religion of my fathers, save in three: the drinking of intoxicants, the wiping over the leather socks, and the abandoning of the recitation of بِسْمِ ٱللَّهِ ٱلرَّحْمَنِ ٱلرَّحِيمِ." Meaning that he himself neither drinks an intoxicant, nor wipes over the socks, nor omits the recitation of بِسْمِ ٱللَّهِ ٱلرَّحْمَنِ ٱلرَّحِيمِ at the head of every surah, in prayer or otherwise, and does not enjoin its omission nor permit it to anyone, whether in a state of dissimulation or not.

The worshipper then recites, after بِسْمِ ٱللَّهِ ٱلرَّحْمَنِ ٱلرَّحِيمِ, the Opening of the Book and a surah, opening the surah too with بِسْمِ ٱللَّهِ ٱلرَّحْمَنِ ٱلرَّحِيمِ, in the two rakahs of Fajr and the first two of Dhuhr, Asr, Maghrib, and the last Isha. For the remaining rakahs nothing particular of the surahs is fixed, and no recitation of the Quran is required in them. Yet it is of merit that one who prays alone lengthen his recitation as far as he is able, though he may shorten it if he wishes; and it is of merit that one who leads a people lighten the prayer, to the measure by which he knows those behind him are able and equal to it, for among them may be the weak and the one in need. The Messenger of Allah rebuked Muadh ibn Jabal over this very thing, when word reached him that Muadh had prayed with a people and lengthened it upon them, and he said to him: "Do not be one who drives people away; pray a prayer suited to the weakest of your companions." The Messenger of Allah would lighten the prayer, while keeping it complete, when he led the people, and would lengthen it when he prayed alone, until his feet would swell from the length of his standing upon them.

"O Messenger of Allah, you are bringing hardship upon yourself, though Allah has forgiven you your past and future sins." He answered, "Should I not then be a thankful servant?" And concerning the measure of the standing in the prayer, what has come from the Ahl al-Bayt is that the longest standing belongs to the Fajr prayer, and after it the Isha, which is less lengthy than Fajr, then the Dhuhr, then the Asr, and then the Maghrib, whose standing is the shortest. No fixed measure is laid down for this.

In an obligatory prayer one should not join two surahs after the Opening of the Book within a single rakah, though there is no harm in doing so in the voluntary prayers. If a worshipper begins one surah and then wishes to recite another, he may turn to the second only before he has passed the midpoint of the one he began, except for Surah al-Jumuah on Friday, Surah al-Ikhlas, and Surah al-Kafirun; once he has begun any of these, he must not break off from it.

The worshipper recites aloud in the Fajr prayer and in the first two rakahs of the Maghrib and the Isha, whether he prays alone or leads others, and he recites quietly in all the rest. In the remaining rakahs, the last two of Dhuhr, Asr, and Isha, and the final rakah of Maghrib, he recites the Opening of the Book alone in a low voice.

After completing the Opening of the Book he should not say "Amin," as the common people do, nor should the one who prays behind the imam say it, for the Messenger of Allah ﷺ forbade that. He and the Imams of his descendants said, "My nation will remain upon the right path of its religion so long as they do not raise a clamor with Amin in their prayer," and the rest of the hadith was mentioned. Jafar ibn Muhammad said that only the Jews used to do this, and it was the ignorant among the people who imitated them in it.

The worshipper should turn to his prayer with his heart and empty his mind for it as far as he is able, for the Messenger of Allah ﷺ said, "When the servant turns toward his prayer, Allah, mighty and majestic, turns toward him with His face and His mercy; and when he turns away from it, Allah turns away from him and entrusts him to His angels." Jafar ibn Muhammad said, "Of the worshipper's prayer only that is raised up which he offered with presence of heart, and this may be a half of it, or a quarter, or less, or more." Yet Allah, mighty and majestic, completes this through the voluntary prayers. All of this is the teaching of the Ahl al-Bayt.

They command the worshipper to be humble in his prayer, to magnify the majesty of Allah within it, and to make his own soul feel this. When Ali ibn al-Husayn wished to enter into prayer, his color would change and he would tremble, so that those present would look at him and ask what had befallen him, and he would say, "I am about to stand before a great King."

They forbade turning about in prayer and letting the gaze wander, and they commanded the worshipper to keep his gaze upon the place of his prostration and to still his limbs as much as he can. There is no harm, if he grows weary, in his shifting his weight from one foot to the other, nor in his attending to some need of his body or his clothing, or reaching for it with his hands; yet if he is able to leave this undone and remain still, that is better. It was narrated of Ali ibn al-Husayn that while he prayed his robe slipped from him and he did not reach out to restore it. When he was asked about this he said, "Do you not know before Whom I was standing, and with Whom I was in intimate speech? By Allah, what I was engaged in occupied me from what you saw."

If a garment falls from a worshipper during prayer and lays bare some part of his nakedness, he must hasten to restore it as far as he can and to cover what has been exposed. The nakedness of a man is what lies between the navel and the knee, while the whole body of a woman is nakedness, save her face and her hands. As for Ali ibn al-Husayn, whose robe fell and who did not replace it, at that time the robe was an outer covering worn over the garment, so that when it fell he was content with the garment beneath it.

The worshipper should not spread his feet apart when he stands in prayer, unless there is some infirmity in him; rather he stands in a balanced posture, leaving between them only a moderate space by which he can stand at ease.

There is no harm in the worshipper's repeating a verse or a word of the Quran by way of admonition and the instilling of awe. If he weeps out of the fear of Allah, this does not sever his prayer; rather there is in it merit and reward. But he should not force weeping upon himself, for such tears are only proper when they come unbidden and he cannot hold them back; if he contrives them, he severs his prayer, and there is in it ostentation, which is detestable.

They said that the worshipper should not break off his recitation and fall silent to attend to something he hears, such as a groan or a cry or the like from someone near him; he should do this for no cause. As for other than this, we have been told that Ibn al-Kawwa entered the mosque in Kufa while the Commander of the Faithful, Ali, was leading the people in prayer and reciting. Ibn al-Kawwa, from behind him, raised his voice with the verse, "And it has already been revealed to you and to those before you: if you associate others with Allah, your work will surely come to nothing, and you will surely be among the losers." So the Commander of the Faithful (ع) fell silent, then resumed his recitation; Ibn al-Kawwa repeated the verse, and he fell silent again. This happened three times. Then the Commander of the Faithful recited, "So be patient; indeed the promise of Allah is true, and let not those who have no certainty of faith unsettle you."

They commanded that the Quran be pondered when the reciter recites it, whether within his prayer or outside of it.

They said that the least by which a prayer is completed, and the smallest measure of it, is the opening of the prayer with the takbir of consecration, the standing for whoever is able, the bowing, the prostration, and the sitting for the tashahhud. Whoever performs these five bounds among the bounds of the prayer, and forgets or does not know what lies beyond them, his prayer is nonetheless complete. Whoever is ignorant of the remaining bounds of the prayer is obliged to learn them and to observe them without delaying or putting it off, and to exert his utmost in learning and memorizing them. Whoever knows the bounds of the prayer is not permitted to leave any of them deliberately, nor to leave off the recitation within it, nor the rest of the takbirs, nor the tashahhud, nor the tasbih, nor to depart from the prayer without the salutation, nor to omit any of its obligatory and its established bounds deliberately. If he does any of that deliberately, he has no prayer, and he is obliged to repeat whatever of the prayer he left in this way.

Whoever prays behind an imam and follows him should make the takbir when the imam makes the takbir, and be silent when the imam recites aloud. He should say, "Our Lord, to You be praise," when the imam says, "Allah hears the one who praises Him," and make the takbir in the same manner throughout the whole prayer, doing this quietly without raising his voice. He performs the tashahhud when the imam performs it and gives the salutation when the imam gives it. And if the imam is one whose state he does not approve of, and behind whom he does not truly take his lead, but prays behind him out of taqiyyah, he opens the prayer for himself, intending that he prays alone and does not take that imam as his leader; he recites, and bows and prostrates along with him without intending thereby to follow him, and he departs when the imam departs, doing in all of this as he would were he praying alone.

Whether he is an imam or not, the worshipper raises his hands when he means to bow, and raises them again when he lifts his head from the bowing, just as he raises them at the takbir of consecration; he does this with the takbir of the bowing and with his saying, "Allah hears the one who praises Him." When he bows, he grasps his knees with his palms and spreads his fingers over them, and he stretches his back evenly until his shoulders and his back are level, for the Messenger of Allah ﷺ used to do this, so that were water poured upon his back it would settle and not run off. He neither raises his head nor lowers it, but keeps it level with his back. In the bowing he says, "Glory be to my Lord, the Magnificent, and praise be to Him," three times, unhurriedly and with composure, whether he is an imam, prays alone, or follows an imam. In the prostration he says, "Glory be to my Lord, the Most High, and exalted is He," likewise three times, without haste, letting his heart feel in all of this the magnifying of Allah, mighty and majestic.

If he prays alone, with no congregation whom he leads, and he adds to the magnifying of Allah in this what he wishes, there is merit in it. It has been narrated of a company of the Imams (عَلَيْهِمُ السَّلَامُ) that before the three glorifications in the bowing they would say, "O Allah, to You have I bowed, to You have I humbled myself, in You have I believed, and to You have I submitted; and You are my Lord. My hearing and my sight, my hair and my skin, my flesh and my blood, my sinews and my bones, and all that my two feet bear up, have humbled themselves to You, not disdaining, nor arrogant, nor weary of Your worship, but in reverence toward You and lowliness before Your obedience. Blessed is Allah, Lord of the worlds." And in the prostration they would say, "To You have I prostrated, to You have I humbled myself, in You have I believed, and to You have I submitted; and You are my Lord. My face has prostrated to the One who created it and fashioned it, who brought forth its hearing and its sight and shaped it in due proportion; blessed is Allah, the best of creators. O Allah, Your forgiveness is wider than my sins and Your mercy is greater than my wrongdoings, so forgive me and have mercy upon me, O Most Merciful of the merciful. There is no god but You, truly and in truth. I have prostrated to You, O Lord, in worship and in servitude; I prostrate to none but You and I worship none, O Lord, but You alone." They would also increase the glorifications of the bowing and the prostration, making them seven, and in this there is merit for the one who does it when he prays alone. As for the one who leads a congregation, we have already mentioned that the sunnah in his case is to make it light while yet completing it.

They said that when the worshipper raises his head from the bowing and says, "Allah hears the one who praises Him," he should say quietly, upon his tongue, "Our Lord, to You be all praise," whether he is an imam or not, and stand upright and erect. If he is an imam he then goes down into prostration, and he does the same if he prays alone. If he prays alone and adds to this, that is good, for it has come down from them that here too there is an addition, as in the bowing and the prostration, and that they would say thereafter, "Praise be to Allah, Lord of the worlds, Possessor of might and majesty, of greatness, of grandeur, and of power. O Allah, forgive me, have mercy upon me, restore me, raise me, and provide for me, for I stand in need of whatever good You send down to me." Then he makes the takbir of the prostration and goes down to prostrate.

When the worshipper goes down to prostrate, he rests upon the inner surface of his palms and spreads them upon the ground, setting them down before his knees; then he kneels upon his knees and prostrates. He holds his elbows somewhat away from his sides, extends his neck, and prostrates upon his forehead and his nose. He does not lay his forearms upon the ground but holds them up, raised from the earth. He draws the fingers of his hands together, with their tips level with his ears yet at a distance from them, and he presses against that upon which he prostrates with the inner surface of his palms, not drawing them into his sleeves.

> لَا يُكَلِّفُ ٱللَّهُ نَفْسًا إِلَّا وُسْعَهَا

When he raises his head from the first prostration, he sits until every joint of his returns to its place. He sets his hands upon his thighs, spreads his left leg beneath his right thigh without sitting upon it, and sets his right leg upright upon its toe if he is able, and if not he lays it upon its side. Then he performs the second prostration, and he sits in this same manner for the tashahhud, both the first and the last. Between the two prostrations he says, "O Allah, forgive me and have mercy upon me." If it is prolonged and he prays alone he says, "O Allah, forgive me, have mercy upon me, restore me, raise me, guide me, pardon me, grant me well-being, and provide for me, for I stand in need of whatever good You send down to me."

When he rises from the first rakah, he rises from the prostration without lowering himself to sit upon the ground. He should not recite anything of the Quran in his bowing or in his prostration. When he departs from the prayer he does so with two salutations, turning his face to the right and to the left, saying with each salutation, "Peace be upon you, and the mercy of Allah and His blessings," intending thereby those around him if he is in a congregation, and the recording angels if he prays alone. When he has finished the prayer and means to rise and go, he turns away to his right. If he is the imam, then when the people have turned to their voluntary prayers he does not walk between the rows, but passes straight through them by an opening, and he does not walk before one who is praying.

## 4. Supplication and the Many Forms of Prayer

On the tashahhud in prayer, on supplication, and on what is longed for when calling upon Allah, the teaching begins with the word of Allah Himself.

> وَقَالَ رَبُّكُمُ ٱدْعُونِىٓ أَسْتَجِبْ لَكُمْۚ إِنَّ ٱلَّذِينَ يَسْتَكْبِرُونَ عَنْ عِبَادَتِى سَيَدْخُلُونَ جَهَنَّمَ دَاخِرِينَ

Allah the Almighty said: ﴿وَقَالَ رَبُّكُمُ ادْعُونِي أَسْتَجِبْ لَكُمْ إِنَّ الَّذِينَ يَسْتَكْبِرُونَ عَنْ عِبَادَتِي سَيَدْخُلُونَ جَهَنَّمَ دَاخِرِينَ﴾, "And your Lord said: Call upon Me; I will answer you. Indeed, those who disdain My worship will enter Hell, humbled" [Ghafir: 60]. And He, blessed and exalted, said: ﴿وَإِذَا سَأَلَكَ عِبَادِي عَنِّي فَإِنِّي قَرِيبٌ أُجِيبُ دَعْوَةَ الدَّاعِ إِذَا دَعَانِ فَلْيَسْتَجِيبُوا لِي وَلْيُؤْمِنُوا بِي لَعَلَّهُمْ يَرْشُدُونَ﴾, "And when My servants ask you concerning Me, indeed I am near. I answer the call of the caller when he calls upon Me. So let them respond to Me and believe in Me, that they may be rightly guided" [al-Baqarah: 186].

We have narrated from the Ahl al-Bayt (عَلَيْهِمُ السَّلَامُ) that the Messenger of Allah (ص) said: "Whoever sits after the second rakah, remembering Allah the Almighty and calling upon Him, Allah honors him. Good deeds are written for him, evil deeds are wiped away from him, and he is raised in degrees, and an angel is charged over him who says to him, Increase in honor, until he departs." And he (ص) said: "By Him in whose hand is the soul of Muhammad, a man's supplication after the Fajr prayer until sunrise is more effective in securing his needs than the one who travels the earth trading with his wealth."

He (ص) said: "Shall I not direct you to a weapon by which Allah will fortify you against your enemy and by which He will make your provision easy?" They said, "Yes, O Messenger of Allah." He said: "You call upon your Lord by night and by day, for the weapon of the believer is supplication."

And he (ص) said: "The most beloved glorification to Allah is the tasbih of talk, سُبْحَة الحديث." It was said, "O Messenger of Allah, and what is the tasbih of talk?" He said: "People are plunged in the world, its vanities and its amusements, and a man seizes that very moment and calls upon Allah the Almighty, and remembers Him, and glorifies Him."

He (ص) said: "Allah the Almighty has not opened for a servant a door of supplication except that He has opened for him the door of response. He has not opened for him a door of seeking forgiveness except that He has opened for him the door of pardon. He has not opened for him a door of gratitude except that He has opened for him the door of increase. And He has not opened for him a door of deed except that He has opened for him the door of acceptance." This is borne witness to by the Book of Allah, whose mention is exalted. Allah, blessed be His name, says: ﴿ادْعُونِي أَسْتَجِبْ لَكُمْ﴾, "Call upon Me; I will answer you" [Ghafir: 60]. And He said: ﴿ثُمَّ يَسْتَغْفِرِ اللَّهَ يَجِدِ اللَّهَ غَفُورًا رَّحِيمًا﴾, "Then seeks the forgiveness of Allah, he will find Allah Forgiving, Merciful" [al-Nisa: 110]. And He said: ﴿لَئِن شَكَرْتُمْ لَأَزِيدَنَّكُمْ﴾, "If you are grateful, I will surely increase you" [Ibrahim: 7]. And He said: ﴿فَمَن يَعْمَلْ مِثْقَالَ ذَرَّةٍ خَيْرًا يَرَهُ﴾, "So whoever does an atom's weight of good will see it" [al-Zalzalah: 7], and He said, "Who is it that will lend Allah a goodly loan?"

The Messenger of Allah (ص) said: "Supplication turns back the decree though it has been firmly ordained," and he clasped his fingers together, palm against palm, joining them all, thumb against thumb and little finger against little finger, as though showing you a thing. He said that an affliction descends toward the servant and comes down to him from the heaven, yet he calls upon Allah and asks Him for well-being, so the supplication ascends into the heaven. The supplication meets the affliction, and the two contend with one another until the Day of Resurrection. And he (ص) said: "Whoever looks upon one stricken with affliction and says, Praise be to Allah who has kept me sound from what He has tried you with, and has favored me over you and over much of His creation with great favor, it becomes a right upon Allah that He not strike him with that affliction."

Jafar ibn Muhammad (ع) said: "Whoever supplicates, let him send prayers upon Muhammad in his supplication, for when the servant supplicates, his supplication is not raised to the heaven until he sends prayers upon the Prophet and upon the people of his house." The Prophet (ص) said: "Place me at the beginning of the supplication, and in its middle, and at its end." And he (ص) said: "Four are made intercessors in the heavens and the earth: Paradise, the Fire, the pure-eyed maidens, and an angel at my head in my grave when I have died. When the servant says, O Allah, I ask You for Paradise, Paradise says, O Allah, make him among my dwellers. When he says, O Allah, deliver me from the Fire, the Fire says, O Allah, deliver him from me. When he says, O Allah, wed me to the pure-eyed maidens, the pure-eyed maidens say, O Allah, wed him to us. And when he says, O Allah, send prayers upon Muhammad and upon the family of Muhammad, the angel at my head says to me, O Muhammad, so-and-so has sent prayers upon you, and Allah says, O Allah, send prayers upon him as he has sent prayers upon Me, so I say, O Allah, send prayers upon him as he has sent prayers upon me."

He (ص) said that the supplication of the believer is answered so long as he is not hasty. It was said, "And how is haste, O Messenger of Allah?" He said: "He supplicates but sees no trace of his supplication, and so he says, I called upon Allah, and Allah has not answered me."

Jafar ibn Muhammad said that Allah the Almighty grants the believing servant whatever He wills of what he asks, and defers of it whatever He wills, according to what is better for him, and Allah the Almighty is truer than any who promises. ﴿وَقَالَ رَبُّكُمُ ادْعُونِي أَسْتَجِبْ لَكُمْ﴾, "And your Lord said: Call upon Me; I will answer you" [Ghafir: 60]. He, exalted be His praise, undertakes the reckoning of His believing servant on the Day of Resurrection, and there appear to the servant good deeds he never performed. It is said to him, "Do you recognize these good deeds?" He says, "No." It is said to him, "You called upon Allah on such a day in such a matter, and He answered you and deferred that until this day, for it was better for you." Then, when the believer sees on that Day the greatness of the reward of his supplication, he says, "O Lord, would that You had hastened nothing of my supplication for me, but had deferred it all to this day."

Then Jafar ibn Muhammad said that Allah raises up, by supplication, the matter which He knows the servant will supplicate for, and He answers it. Were it not for Allah's granting the servant that supplication, an affliction would have struck him that would have uprooted him from the face of the earth. There is no Muslim who calls upon Allah but that He answers him, or turns away from him by his supplication an affliction greater than what he asked, or stores it for him until a day and a time in which it is more beneficial for him, so long as he does not supplicate for wrongdoing or the severing of kinship. He (ع) said: "Supplication turns back the decree though it has been firmly ordained, so multiply supplication, for it is the key to every good and the success of every need. What is with Allah is not attained except by supplication, and there is no door that is knocked upon much but that it is soon opened to the one who knocks."

Abu Jafar Muhammad ibn Ali (ع) was asked by a man about the saying of the Messenger of Allah (ص) concerning the black seed, which is al-shuniz. He said, "Yes, the Messenger of Allah (ص) said of it, In it is a cure for every disease save death. But shall I direct you to that for which the Messenger of Allah (ص) gave you no cure?" The man said, "And what is it?" He said: "Supplication, for it turns back the decree though it has been firmly ordained," and Abu Jafar ibn Ali clasped his fingers and joined them before him at his words "though it has been firmly ordained." Then he said: "There is nothing more beloved to Allah the Almighty than that He be asked, and nothing turns back the decree except supplication."

He was asked about the word of Allah the Almighty, ﴿فَإِذَا فَرَغْتَ فَانصَبْ وَإِلَى رَبِّكَ فَارْغَب﴾, "So when you have finished, then labor, and to your Lord direct your longing" [al-Sharh: 7-8]. He said: "That is supplication after the obligatory prayer. Beware of forsaking it, for its excellence over the rest of supplication is like the excellence of the obligatory prayer over the voluntary." And he said: "Supplication is before the prayer, and within the prayer, and after the prayer." And he said: "Supplication after the obligatory prayer is more excellent than the voluntary prayer." Jafar ibn Muhammad said: "Supplication is worship," and he recited the word of Allah the Almighty: ﴿ادْعُونِي أَسْتَجِبْ لَكُمْ إِنَّ الَّذِينَ يَسْتَكْبِرُونَ عَنْ عِبَادَتِي سَيَدْخُلُونَ جَهَنَّمَ دَاخِرِينَ﴾, "Call upon Me; I will answer you. Indeed, those who disdain My worship will enter Hell, humbled" [Ghafir: 60]. Then he said: "It, by Allah, is worship."

When he prayed the two rakahs of Fajr, he would call with this supplication, saying: "I have taken hold of the firmest handhold of Allah, which has no breaking, and I have clung to the strong rope of Allah. I seek refuge in Allah from the evil of the devils of mankind and jinn all together. I seek refuge in Allah from the evil of the corrupt among the Arabs and the non-Arabs. Allah is sufficient for me. I rely upon Allah. I lean my back upon Allah. I seek my need from Allah. There is no might nor power except in Allah. O Allah, make for me a light in my heart, a light in my hearing, a light in my sight, a light in my tongue, a light in my hair, a light in my skin, a light in my flesh, a light in my blood, a light in my bone, a light in my sinews, a light before me, a light behind me, a light on my right, a light on my left, a light above me, a light beneath me, and a light in my grave. O Allah, make my light great, and abundant, and full of joy, and a blessing, and a delight."

Then he recites five verses from the end of Surah Al Imran, from "Indeed, in the creation of the heavens and the earth" to His word, "You do not break the appointment." Then he says, three times: "Glory be to the Lord of the morning, the Cleaver of the dawn, who made the night and the day a repose, and the sun and the moon a reckoning." Then he says: "O Allah, make the first of this day of mine righteousness, and its middle prosperity, and its last success. O Allah, whoever wakes with his need and his request directed to a creature among Your creation, my need and my request are to You alone, who has no partner." Then he recites the Verse of the Throne and the two suras of refuge, and then he says, one hundred times: "Glory be to my Lord the Magnificent, and by His praise. I seek the forgiveness of Allah and turn to Him in repentance." If this is missed, he makes it up.

He said that the worshipper says in the first tashahhud: "In the name of Allah, the Most Gracious, the Most Merciful. In the name of Allah, and by Allah, and all the beautiful names belong to Allah. I bear witness that there is no god but Allah, alone, having no partner, and I bear witness that Muhammad is His servant and His messenger (ص), and the Imams from the people of his house, the righteous." Then he rises. And when he sits in the last tashahhud, in which he gives the salutation and from which he departs, he says: "In the name of Allah, and by Allah, and all the beautiful names belong to Allah. The greetings belong to Allah, and the pure prayers, the good, the untainted, the tender, the ample, the morning-going, the flowing, belong to Allah. Whatever is good and pure and increasing and honored and exalted and refined belongs to Allah. I bear witness that there is no god but Allah, alone, having no partner, and I bear witness that Muhammad is His servant and His messenger, the excellent Messenger and the excellent Guardian, and that Paradise is true, and the Fire is true, and the Resurrection is true, and that the Hour is coming, no doubt in it, and that Allah will raise up those who are in the graves. O Allah, send prayers upon Muhammad and upon the family of Muhammad, and bless and have mercy upon Muhammad and the family of Muhammad, as You sent prayers and mercy and blessing upon Ibrahim and upon the family of Ibrahim in all the worlds; indeed You are Praiseworthy, Glorious. O Allah, forgive me and my parents and have mercy upon us as they raised me when I was small." And he said that one whose parents are Muslim says this, and adds: "And forgive us and our brothers who preceded us in faith, and place not in our hearts any rancor toward those who believe; indeed You are Kind, Merciful." Then he supplicates with whatever he loves of supplication for this world and the hereafter, then he sends prayers upon the Prophet and upon his family and says: "Peace be upon you, O Prophet, and the mercy of Allah and His blessings. Peace be upon us and upon the righteous servants of Allah."

The whole of the tashahhud he says under his breath, uttering it with his tongue in a slight utterance. Then he gives the salutation, and he announces the salutation aloud if he is the imam, and lowers his voice with it if he is behind an imam. If he raises it a little, there is no harm in that. And if he is alone, he may lower his voice if he wishes and announce it if he wishes. When he rises from the first tashahhud, he leans upon his hands and says quietly, "O Allah, by Your power I rise and I sit," and he pronounces the takbir for standing. When the worshipper has given the salutation from the obligatory prayer, he turns the backs of his hands upon his thighs and raises them if he wishes, and that is more excellent, placing the inner surfaces of his palms toward his face, and he recites and says: "O Allah, I draw near to You through You, and through Muhammad Your Prophet, and Ali Your friend, and Fatima al-Zahra the daughter of Your Prophet, and al-Hasan and al-Husayn, and Ali ibn al-Husayn, and Muhammad ibn Ali, and Jafar ibn Muhammad," and he names the Imams one by one down to the Imam of the Age. Then he says: "O Allah, I draw near to You through them and turn toward You by them, so make me through them honored in Your sight in this world and the hereafter, and among those brought near, and make certainty firm in my heart, and increase me in guidance and light." Then he supplicates with whatever he wishes.

Abu Jafar Muhammad ibn Ali said: "When you rise to the prayer, or enter the mosque intending the prayer, say: In the name of Allah, and by Allah, and from Allah, and to Allah, and as Allah wills, and there is no might nor power except in Allah. O Allah, make me among Your visitors and among those who frequent Your mosques. Open for me, O Lord, the door of Your forgiveness, and close against me the doors of Your disobedience. Praise be to Allah who has made me among those who commune with Him. O Allah, turn toward me with Your Face; exalted is Your praise and blessed is Your name. Then begin the prayer."

Ali (ع) said: "Whoever would be pleased to be measured with a full measure that falls not short, let him say, when he has turned away from his prayer: Glory be to your Lord, the Lord of Might, above what they describe; and peace be upon the messengers; and praise be to Allah, Lord of the worlds." And he said: "I heard the Messenger of Allah (ص) say: Whoever recites after every prescribed prayer, "Say: He is Allah, One," one hundred times, will cross the Sirat on the Day of Resurrection, and on his right will be eight cubits and on his left eight cubits, and Gabriel will take hold of his waistband while he gazes into the Fire to his right and to his left; and whoever he sees within it, among those he knows who entered by a sin other than shirk, he takes him by the hand and enters him into Paradise by his intercession." This, and Allah knows best, is a report given in summary, and it is particular, not general, and the intercession is not for all people, so that whoever performs this recitation would have earned what he (ص) said. Rather, intercession belongs only to the one whom Allah the Almighty has appointed for it, "and they possess no power of intercession except one who has taken with the Most Merciful a covenant."

Ali said: "Whoever sits in the place in which he prayed the Fajr prayer, after he has prayed it, and recites within it, Say: He is Allah, One, eleven times before the sun rises, no sin will follow him that day, though Satan strive hard against him." And he said: "The Messenger of Allah (ص) said to me, O Ali, recite after every prayer the Verse of the Throne, for none guards it faithfully but a prophet, or a truthful one, or a martyr."

Jafar ibn Muhammad said: "Whoever glorifies with the tasbih of Fatima (ع) before he folds his legs from the obligatory prayer, after he has given the salutation, is forgiven; and he begins with the takbir." He said, and the tasbih of Fatima is: "Allah is the Greatest, and Glory be to Allah, and Praise be to Allah," thirty-three times of each, which he seals with "There is no god but Allah," so that it comes to one hundred. He said that the Messenger of Allah (ص) taught this tasbih to Fatima when she came asking him for a servant. He said: "O Fatima, this is better for you than a servant, and a servant, and a servant, and than all that is in the world. You say it after every prescribed prayer, and one hundred good deeds are written for you, and the good deed is tenfold its like, so that becomes a thousand." And whoever says one hundred times, "Glory be to Allah, and praise be to Allah, and I seek the forgiveness of Allah, and there is no god but Allah, and Allah is the Greatest, and as Allah wills, and there is no might nor power except in Allah; Allah is sufficient for me, I have taken refuge in Allah, I have relied upon Allah."

None knows what reward such a person has with God, whose name be exalted, save God alone; and the least of it is ten thousand good deeds, for God multiplies a single good deed tenfold, as He has said. It is recommended that whoever rises from the prayer, on leaving it, utter the takbir three times and then say: "There is no god but God, alone, with no partner; to Him belongs the kingdom and to Him belongs praise; in His hand is all good, and He is able to do all things. There is no god but God alone; He fulfilled His promise, gave victory to His servant, and defeated the confederates alone; to Him belongs the kingdom and to Him belongs praise. Praise be to God, Lord of the Worlds. There is no god but God, and God is greatest; glory be to God, and praise be to God." This is said ten times.

Ali used to say after every prescribed prayer: "Then by Your light You guided, so to You belongs praise; Your forbearance is great, so You pardoned, and to You belongs praise; You stretched out Your hands and gave, so to You belongs praise. Our Lord, Your face is the most generous of faces, Your station the best of stations, and Your gift the most beneficial and the most pleasant of gifts. You are obeyed, our Lord, and You give thanks; You are disobeyed, our Lord, and You forgive. You answer the supplication of the distressed, heal the sick, and deliver from affliction. Our Lord, You accept repentance and forgive sins for whom You will; none can requite Your favor, and no speaker can number Your due." Abu Abd Allah Jafar ibn Muhammad said: "Because this is a remembrance of God as He deserves to be remembered a thousandfold, whoever says it will have a thousand good deeds."

It is right and recommended, too, to say after the prayer: "O God, for You I have prayed, to You I have called, and in You I have hoped; so I ask You to place in my prayer and my supplication a blessing by which You expiate my sins, whiten my face, honor my standing, and lift my burden from me. O God, put down my burden, and make what is with You better for me. Praise be to God, who has discharged from me a prayer that was ordained upon me and upon the believers as a timed obligation." And Abu Jafar Muhammad ibn Ali said: "The least that will suffice you in supplication after the obligatory prayer is to say: O God, I ask You for every good that Your knowledge encompasses, and I seek refuge in You from every evil that Your knowledge encompasses. O God, I ask You for well-being in all my affairs, and I seek refuge in You from the disgrace of this world and the punishment of the Hereafter."

### The desirable forms of supplication

There is no appointed time for supplication. Whoever wishes to pray may do so, asking God for his needs and calling upon Him for whatever he loves, so long as he does not call for a wrong or for the severing of kinship. It is fitting for one who would supplicate that he set a righteous deed before his supplication, that he repent to God of his sins, that he clear himself of the claims standing between him and others of God's creation, and that he make his deed and his intention sincerely for God. For it is related of the Messenger of God (ص) that he said: "One who supplicates without action is like one who draws a bow without a string." And Abu Dharr (رضي الله عنه) said: "Righteous conduct is sufficient accompaniment for supplication, as salt is sufficient seasoning for food."

Whoever would supplicate should begin by praising God his Lord and extolling Him as He deserves, and by blessing Muhammad His prophet and his family; then let him make his petition, and let him bless the prophet and his family in the middle of his supplication and at its end. For the Messenger of God (ص) heard a man supplicating before he had praised God, and said: "This man has been hasty with his Lord." And when he wished to supplicate he would begin by praising God, then say: "O God, there is none who can withhold what You give, and none who can give what You withhold; none who can constrain what You expand, and none who can expand what You constrain; none who can bring forward what You defer, and none who can defer what You bring forward. O God, You are the Forbearing, so You are never rash; You are the Bountiful, so You are never niggardly; You are the Mighty, so You are never abased; You are the Impregnable, so You are never assailed; You are the Possessor of Majesty and Honor; and there is no power and no strength save in God, the Most High, the Almighty."

He also said: "Do not make me like the rider's cup." For the rider fills his cup and sets it aside, and when he has finished with his gear and his need he looks: if he has need to drink he drinks, or if he has need to make ablution he makes ablution, and otherwise he pours it out. "Place me at the beginning of the supplication, and in the middle of the supplication, and at the end of the supplication." He meant by this that the blessing upon him and his family should not stand at the end of the supplication only, but should stand at its beginning, its middle, and its end.

When God sent down the verse commanding the believers to bless the prophet, the people said: "O Messenger of God, you have taught us how to greet you; how, then, are we to bless you?" He said: "Say: O God, bless Muhammad and the family of Muhammad, as You blessed Ibrahim and the family of Ibrahim; You are Praiseworthy, Glorious."

> فَمَا ٱسْتَكَانُوا۟ لِرَبِّهِمْ وَمَا يَتَضَرَّعُونَ

Ali was asked about the saying of God, ﴾فَمَا اسْتَكَانُوا لِرَبِّهِمْ وَمَا يَتَضَرَّعُونَ﴿, "They did not humble themselves to their Lord, nor did they supplicate" (al-Muminun: 76). He said: "They were not lowly in their supplication to God, nor were they sincere in it."

Supplications are more than can be numbered or contained in a book, yet we mention some of that whose answering may be hoped for, as it has come to us from the Ahl al-Bayt, from the reports and the encouragement to it. Among this is what was related of the Messenger of God (ص), that he said: "There are ninety-nine names; whoever numbers them enters Paradise." This tradition has been narrated often, and men have differed over these names and been at sea concerning them. Sufyan al-Thawri set them down in his compendium, and named names other than those handed down from the Imams (عَلَيْهِمُ السَّلَامُ), naming them at variance with what they gave. Ali ibn al-Husayn (ع) was asked about them, and he described their excellence and the answering of the supplication of whoever prays by them, and magnified their standing in words too long to relate. He took a covenant of whoever learned them of him that he would teach them only to one he trusted, and would not supplicate God by them for any wrong or for the severing of His kinship. He said that they are in the Book of God, whose mention is exalted.

In the Opening of the Book are five names: By God, O Lord, O Most Merciful, O Ever Compassionate, O King. In Surah al-Baqarah are twenty-six: O Encompassing, O All-Powerful, O All-Knowing, O Wise, O Ever-Relenting, O Patient, O Vast, O Originator, O Hearing, O Sufficient, O Knowing, O Kind, O Grateful, O God, O One, O Forgiving, O Forbearing, O Withholder, O Expander, O Living, O Self-Subsisting, O Most High, O Great, O Rich, O Guardian, O Praiseworthy. In Surah Al Imran are four: O Ever-Standing, O Bestower, O Swift in Reckoning, O Aware. In Surah al-Nisa: O Watchful, O Witness, O Pardoning, O Nourisher, O Trustee. In Surah al-Anam are five: O Fashioner, O Subduer, O Able, O Subtle, O Ruler. In Surah al-Araf are two: O Giver of Life, O Giver of Death. In Surah al-Anfal are two: O Best of Protectors, O Best of Helpers. In Surah Hud are seven: O Preserver, O Near, O Answerer, O Strong, O Glorious, O Loving, O Doer of what He wills. In Surah al-Ra'd are two: O Great, O Most Exalted. In Surah Ibrahim is one: O Ever-Giving. In Surah al-Hujurat is one: O Ever-Creating. In Surah al-Hajj is one: O Raiser of the dead. In Surah al-Anbiya are two: O Truthful, O Inheritor. In Surah al-Muminun is one: O Generous. In Surah al-Nur: O Guide. In Surah Saba is one: O Opener. In Surah al-Mumin are four: O Forgiver, O Accepter of repentance, O Severe in punishment, O Possessor of Bounty. In Surah al-Dhariyat are three: O Provider, O Possessor of Strength, O Firm. In Surah al-Tur is one: O Ever-Kind. In Surah Iqtarabat is one: O Omnipotent. In Surah al-Rahman are three: O Everlasting, O Possessor of Majesty and Honor. In Surah al-Hadid are four: O First, O Last, O Manifest, O Hidden. In Surah al-Hashr are ten: O Holy, O Peace, O Granter of Security, O Overseer, O Mighty, O Compeller, O Supreme, O Creator, O Maker, O Fashioner. In Surah al-Buruj are two: O Beginner, O Restorer. In Surah al-Ikhlas are two: O One, O Eternal Refuge.

These are the names of God, exalted is His mention, in His Book: ninety-nine names, and with "By God" a hundred. By them, when God is called upon He answers, and when He is asked He gives. A company of the believers who prayed by them with sincerity and certainty have known the blessing of their being answered; their mention is attested and their excellence handed down.

> إِنَّ ٱلَّذِينَ آمَنُوا۟ وَعَمِلُوا۟ ٱلصَّلِحَتِ سَيَجْعَلُ لَهُمُ ٱلرَّحْمَنُ وُدًّۭا

The Messenger of God (ص) said to Ali: "O Ali, shall I not teach you words which, were your sins upon you like the foam of the sea, God would put away from you? Say: O God, fix for me affection in the breasts of the believers, and make for me with You a covenant," or, "make for me with You affection." Then God sent down: ﴾إِنَّ الَّذِينَ آمَنُوا وَعَمِلُوا الصَّالِحَاتِ سَيَجْعَلُ لَهُمُ الرَّحْمَنُ وُدًّا﴿, "Verily those who believe and do righteous deeds, the All-Merciful will appoint for them affection" (Maryam: 96). And this was among the supplications recited concerning him.

There was a supplication of Ali, the supreme supplication of the Imams, by which he would pray in weighty matters. He would say: O God, I seek from You the grace to thank You well; I ask of You the gift of a tongue unloosed in proclaiming Your praise; and I desire from You to be set right in what earns Your increase. I seek refuge in You from that which calls down the visitation of other than You and brings on the sudden strokes that befall the like of me. O God, You began with me by the most abundant of gifts and singled me out for them above much of Your creation, so that, without my asking, there fell to me from Your apportioning the most fortunate share and the fullest lot, when You made me among the people of Your knowledge and those who hold fast to the rope of Your oneness, before the passing of ages, the elapsing of times, and the running of the eras, before the creation of male and female, the multiplying of generations, and the succession of descendants. Then You created me in the finest form and the most complete image, and made me hearing, seeing, speaking, understanding, discerning. Then You set my frame straight and completed my faculties, and did not deprive me of any of my limbs, but fortified them against afflictions by Your power and kept them from the stillness of incapacity by Your governance. As You have taken it upon Yourself, O my God, to repel maladies from my body by my health and to guard my weakness, so bless Muhammad and the family of Muhammad, as You are worthy and as they are worthy of You. Remove from my soul the hindrance of sloth and the discouragement of delay; release it from the grip of weariness and loose from it the fetters of listlessness; rouse it to bear the burdens with which You have charged it, and set it upon the bounds of Your sacred limits.

O God, loose my tongue with the abundance of Your remembrance, the goodness of thanking You, and the repeating of Your favors; make it flourish with the recitation of Your Book at every hour of night and day; make it prefer truth in hope of its benefit and forsake falsehood in fear of its harm; restrain it from sharing in the backbiting of the backbiters and from chewing over the honor of the believers; and silence it from the stabbing of slanderers and the perjury of every contemptible swearer. O God, spread out my hand in giving, freely and without stint, from spending in Your way; a hand that seeks Your mercy for those who hope in You, not inclining, in Your displeasure, toward hoarding, nor preferring, when Your good pleasure is within reach, riches over open-handedness. O God, when I have drawn down the treasures of Your abundant storehouses and prayed for the rain of Your gifts in mercy, do not turn my two hands away disappointed of the grant of Your bestowal; rather bind their rising to You to the descent of Your aid and the swiftness of Your answer, and hold them back from touching what the hands of those who tamper with Your sanctities and transgress Your command have long reached for. Let my eyes pour down, O God, with the flowing of tears and the streaming of weeping out of fear of You, and lower them from the moments You have forbidden them; make them humble before what may adorn them of the trinkets of this world and its flowers; and guard them, O God, from the drowsiness of slumber and the sweetness of sleep when the heedless lie down upon their beds. Bar my hearing, O God, from giving ear to what does not please You of the speech of the erring, and make it convey to my heart that which draws me near to You and earns Your good pleasure over me: the holding-lawful of Your lawful, the holding-forbidden of Your forbidden, good conduct at Your doors, and longing for the voluntary acts by which one hastens to Your good pleasure. Empty my heart, O God, of every breach in which I was heedless of Your command; take it back from what earns Your displeasure; and pour into it a full measure of Your love, that it may drive it to the nearness of intimate discourse with You and impel it to long for what is with You, of that which You have prepared for Your friends in the abode of Your honor. Question it about what has slipped away of the wreckage of this world, so weighty in the eyes of its people. Set my feet firm for You, O God, in the standing of devotion for Your intimate discourse, and in knocking at the door of Your answer, a door opened only to one who turns his face toward Your face; and aid me by them to recite Your Book at every hour of night and day, and grant them the strength of Your increase when I journey to You upon them through the pitch-dark of the far-off nights, that You may fulfill Your promise. Leave in me no limb but that I have made it ready for its task, no aim but that I have accomplished it, no rising resolve but that I have made it firm, no thread I mean to twist upon right guidance but that I have made it fast, and no craving of greed but that I have crushed it. O God, whatever failing has held me back from attaining a good benefit hoped for from none but You, and whatever visiting misfortune is lifted only by humble entreaty drawing near to You, bring me to it by Your bounty and Your generosity; for You do whatever You will and decree whatever You wish, and You are the most merciful of the merciful. So deal with me in all my states as befits You, by Muhammad and the family of Muhammad, the truthful, upon whom be greetings and peace all together, in well-being, O Lord of the Worlds.

He would say in his supplication: O Allah, You are more worthy of what befits You of Your pardon than I am of what befits me of Your punishment. O Allah, honor Muhammad and the family of Muhammad, as You are worthy of them and they are worthy of You. Do not discipline me with Your punishment, do not scheme against me with Your cunning, and do not lead me astray from where I do not know. O Allah, whoever is bettered by Your grace prospers, and whoever errs by his own sin perishes. No benefactor can dispense with Your support and Your aid, and no wrongdoer overcomes You, nor does any affair pass beyond Your power. So do with me what is worthy of You, for You are worthy of every good. Then he would pray for whatever he wished.

And it was among his supplications: O Lord, how many an enemy has drawn against me the sword of his aggression and honed for me the edge of his blade, sharpened the point of his resolve against me, and mixed for me his deadly poisons. His watchful eye never slept over me, and he resolved to visit me with harm and to make me drink its bitterness. Seeing my weakness before such heavy blows, and my helplessness to take revenge upon the one who set out to war against me, and seeing that I stood alone among the many who bore me enmity, You reinforced me with Your victory over him and strengthened me. You scattered his gathering after it had assembled, and raised my standing above his, and turned back upon him the arrows he had aimed at me. You choked him with his own rage and struck him with his own dart, so that he was thrown back upon himself after his arrogance, and humbled and brought low after his overbearing. Servile and captive he lay in the snare of his own rope, that very snare in which he had felt sure he would see me on the day of his triumph. To You be praise, as You are worthy of it, powerful and never overcome, forbearing and never hasty.

This is a supplication that Ali taught to al-Husayn. He described to him such of its excellence that Allah, mighty and majestic, has made the reward binding upon whoever supplicates with it, a merit too long to recount. He took from him a covenant not to teach it to anyone save his family, his Shia, and his loyal ones, and al-Husayn pledged him upon that. He said to him: When you wish to ask Allah, mighty and majestic, for a need, or to call upon Him for any affair of this world or the hereafter, then say:

Glory be to Allah, praise be to Allah, there is no god but Allah, Allah is the greatest, and there is no power nor strength except by Allah. Glory be to Allah in the depths of the night and at the ends of the day. Glory be to Allah in the morning and in the evening. Glory be to Allah when you enter upon evening and when you enter upon morning. To Him is praise in the heavens and the earth, and in the late afternoon, and when you reach midday. He brings the living out of the dead and brings the dead out of the living, and He revives the earth after its death, and thus shall you be brought forth. Glory be to your Lord, the Lord of Might, above what they ascribe to Him. Peace be upon the messengers, and praise be to Allah, the Lord of the worlds. Glory be to the Owner of dominion and sovereignty. Glory be to the Owner of might, greatness, and compelling majesty. Glory be to the Living who does not die. Glory be to the King, the Most Holy. Glory be to the Everlasting, the Abiding. Glory be to the Living, the Self-Sustaining. Glory be to the High, the Most High. Exalted and sanctified is He, all-glorious and all-holy, our Lord and the Lord of the angels and the Spirit. Glory be to our Lord the Most High, exalted and sanctified is He.

O Allah, I have entered upon evening and upon morning within Your favor and Your well-being, so complete Your favor and Your well-being upon me, and grant me to render You Your thanks. O Allah, by Your light I was guided, by Your grace I seek help, and within Your favor I have entered evening and morning. O Allah, I call You to witness, and You suffice as a witness, and I call to witness Your angels, the bearers of Your throne, and all Your creation, that You are Allah, there is no god but You alone, having no partner, and that Muhammad is Your servant and Your messenger, may Allah bless him and his family. O Allah, record for me this testimony with You until I meet it on the Day of Resurrection, while You are pleased with me, for You are powerful over all things.

O Allah, to You be praise, a praise before which the sky lowers its shelter and by which the earth and all upon it glorify You. O Allah, to You be praise, a praise whose beginning ascends and whose end is never exhausted. O Allah, to You be praise, everlasting, abiding, without end. And to You be praise upon me, within me, and with me, and with my child, whether I sleep or wake, and when I have died and passed away and You alone remain, O my Master. O Allah, to You be praise with all the praises that are Yours, for all the blessings that are Yours. O Allah, to You be praise upon every vein at rest and every vein that throbs. To You be praise upon every morsel and every drink, every grasp of the hand, and upon every place where a hair grows. O Allah, to You be praise for Your forbearance after Your knowledge, and to You be praise for Your pardon after Your power. To You be praise, the Sender of praise. To You be praise, the Inheritor of praise. To You be praise, the Originator of praise. To You be praise, the Bestower of praise. To You be praise, the Ancient of praise, Raiser of ranks, Sender down of blessings from above the seven heavens, who brings out of the darknesses into the light, who changes evil deeds into good deeds and makes good deeds into ranks. O Allah, to You be praise, Forgiver of sin, Accepter of repentance, Severe in punishment, Lord of bounty, there is no god but He, to Him is the return. O Allah, to You be praise in the night when it covers over, and to You be praise in the day when it shines forth, and to You be praise in the hereafter and the first. O Allah, to You be praise to the number of the dust, the pebbles, the seeds, of mankind and jinn, of birds, beasts, and wild things. To You be praise to the number of what Your book has counted. To You be praise for all that Your knowledge has encompassed, a praise abundant, wholesome, and blessed.

Then he says: There is no god but Allah alone, having no partner. His is the dominion and His is the praise. He gives life and gives death, and He is Living and does not die. In His hand is all good, and He is powerful over all things, ten times.

Then he says: I seek forgiveness of Allah, other than whom there is no god, the Living, the Self-Sustaining, for all my sins, and I repent to Him, ten times.

Then he says: O Allah, O Most Gracious, O Most Merciful, ten times. Then he says: O Compassionate, O Bestower, ten times. Then he says: O Originator of the heavens and the earth, O Owner of majesty and honor, ten times. Then he says: In the name of Allah, the Most Gracious, the Most Merciful, ten times. Then he says: Amin, Amin, ten times. Then ask your need, and pray upon the Prophet and upon his family.

Ali ibn al-Husayn would supplicate in the heart of the night, after every eye had grown still, saying in his supplication: My God, the stars of Your heavens have set, the eyes of Your creation have slept, and the voices of Your servants have fallen silent. The kings of Banu Umayyah have shut their doors upon themselves, set their guards to circle them, and veiled themselves from any who would ask them for a need or seek from them a benefit. But You, my God, are Living, Self-Sustaining. Neither drowsiness nor sleep takes hold of You, and nothing occupies You away from anything else. The doors of Your heavens stand open to whoever calls upon You, and Your treasuries are not withheld from whoever asks of You, but are freely given. My God, You are the Generous who turns away no petitioner among the believers who asks of You, nor veils Yourself from any among them who seeks You. No, by Your might, their needs are not intercepted short of You, nor are they fulfilled by any other than You.

O Allah, You see me standing here, and You know my inmost secret. You look upon what is in my heart and upon what would set me right in my hereafter and my world. So come to me in this with Your bounty and Your generosity, my God. You see me awaiting death, and it is the appointed meeting and the standing before You. It has spoiled for me my food and my drink, choked my throat, driven sleep from my pillow and my rest, and kept me from repose. My God, how shall he sleep who fears the sudden coming of the angel of death in the watches of the night and the watches of the day? Rather, how shall the wise man sleep when the angel of death does not sleep in his vigil over him, seeking to seize his soul in the morning, in the depths of night, and in whatever hour it may be?

At these words he would weep, and would sob until his family and his loyal ones took fright at his weeping. They would come to him and find that he had pressed his cheek to the dust, saying: My Lord, I ask You for rest and repose at the hour of death, and for the passage to the abode of settled rest.

And among the supplications of Abu Abdullah Jafar ibn Muhammad: O Allah, bless Muhammad and the family of Muhammad, and have mercy on my lowliness and my standing before You, my humble entreaty to You, my estrangement from people, and my intimacy with You. O my God, do not disgrace me, for You know me well, and do not chastise me, for You are able over me. O Allah, I seek refuge in You from abasement in life, from an evil sight at death, from an ill return in the grave, and from remorse on the Day of Resurrection. O Allah, I ask You for a life of contentment, a death of ease, and a return that is honorable, neither disgracing nor shaming. O Allah, Your forgiveness is wider than my sins, and Your mercy is more to be hoped for by me than my own deeds, so forgive me and have mercy on me. O Allah, to You be praise if I obey You, and Yours is the proof against me if I disobey You. No good deed of mine or of any other is ever lost with You, and there is no excuse for me or for any other in wrongdoing. O Allah, help me in my religion by my worldly life, and in my hereafter by my piety. O Allah, guard me in what I am absent from, and do not entrust me to myself in what I am present for. O You whom forgiveness does not diminish and whom sins do not harm, bless Muhammad and the family of Muhammad, forgive me what does not harm You, and grant me what does not lessen You. O Allah, I ask You for ampleness in provision, for ease, forgiveness, mercy, health, piety, pardon, well-being, sufficiency, contentment, moderation, righteousness, gratitude, and success in all my affairs, for the hereafter and for this world, by the ease of all things and not by their hardship, O Generous One. My Lord, extend that same favor to my child, my family, my kin, and my brothers among the believing men and women and the Muslim men and women, the living of them and the dead, whom You have loved and drawn near, and to those whom I have begotten and those who begot me. O Allah, I ask You for patience in obeying You and patience away from disobeying You, for contentment with Your decree, for truthfulness in every place, for the realities of faith, and for gratitude for Your blessings that cannot be numbered. O Allah, bless, You and Your angels, Muhammad Your prophet and all the family of Muhammad.

An account of what is permissible and what is not permissible of speech and action within the prayer

It has been related to us from the Ahl al-Bayt that speech, and the breaking of ablution, and turning away wholly from the Qiblah, and whatever is gross in action, all cut off the prayer. But supplication, glorification, the takbir, the recitation of the Quran, and whatever a servant addresses to his Lord within the prayer are not so. Though these be speech, they are not the speech that cuts off the prayer. Abu Jafar Muhammad ibn Ali said, concerning that with which a man addresses Allah in prayer, that it is speech which does not cut off the prayer. They said: whoever is greeted with peace while he is in prayer, if he returns the greeting within himself or by a slight gesture, does not thereby cut off his prayer. They did not hold that a gesture, or a word said within oneself, is among the things that cut off the prayer. Yet to leave that aside and to turn wholly to the prayer is better. So if the one greeted leaves it until he has finished his prayer and returns the greeting after he has given the closing salaam, if he wishes, that is better.

They gave leave to one who sees a matter he must forbid, or fears something, or sees that he is about to miss something, to glorify Allah by saying "Glory be to Allah" while he prays, intending it as part of the Quran, since it is from it, or intending it as glorification, since that is permitted within the prayer. If the matter is one that carries no great harm, then to turn to his prayer and leave it aside is better. A gesture indicating what the worshipper intends is likewise in this same sense: if he points to one he wishes to address without turning his face from the Qiblah, there is no harm in it, though if he leaves it and turns to his prayer, that is better, if he finds in that some good.

They related that laughter in the prayer cuts it off, but that a smile does not cut it off, though it is disliked for the worshipper. They forbade blowing within the prayer, as when the worshipper blows upon the place of his prostration should he see upon it something he dislikes to prostrate over. If it is of what may not be prostrated upon, there is no harm in his removing it with his hand. If there is something in his place of prayer, let him move it aside, and if it is on the ground, let him move it beneath his feet, and if he takes it away with a garment, that is better. The worshipper should not spit toward his face nor to his right, but let him spit to his left, and let him not turn his face from the Qiblah. They forbade the cracking of the fingers in the prayer, which is that the worshipper bends them so that they crackle. This is of idle play and heedlessness in the prayer, and it cuts it off, since he who does it has no need of it. But if they crack from an act he did not intend, or from something he could not avoid, there is no harm in that.

They gave leave to scratch within the prayer for one to whom an itch comes, if he cannot bear it patiently and it distracts him from his prayer. They gave leave to the worshipper to kill a snake or scorpion he sees in the prayer, and every fearsome, harmful creature, and to kill a louse, a flea, or a gnat he finds upon his body or his garment while he is in prayer. They forbade deliberately toying with the hand upon the beard or elsewhere in the prayer, but gave leave for that if it happen without intent. They gave leave to recite from a written Quran in the prayer, and forbade stretching and yawning in the prayer. Whoever is overtaken by that without intending it does not thereby cut off his prayer. Likewise a sneeze does not cut off the prayer, though let the worshipper make it light if he is able, cover his mouth with his hand, praise Allah, and bless Muhammad and his family.

They forbade the worshipper to wipe his forehead of dust or of anything else he prostrates upon while he is in the prayer, but he may wipe that away when he has turned from the prayer, and let him not leave it. They forbade the tawarruk in the prayer, which is that the worshipper sets his hand upon his hip. And they forbade a man to pray with his face veiled, save from a necessity or an ailment.

As for the garments a person wears in prayer, the Messenger of God (ص) said: "Let every one of you fear God in his garment during his prayers." For this reason one should pray in the finest clothing he can find, for prayer is the best occasion on which a person adorns himself. We have narrated from the Ahl al-Bayt that they permitted praying in a single garment, provided it covers the worshipper so that his awrah is not visible. If it is so light that it reveals him, they did not hold it sufficient for him to wear alone; he must have with it something that conceals the awrah. For a man, the awrah is what lies between the navel and the knees. For a woman, her whole body is awrah that she must cover in prayer, except her face. A free woman, if she wishes to pray, veils herself; a slave woman may pray without a veil, but she covers her head and does not leave her hair exposed. It is permissible for a man to pray in a wrapper that covers his awrah even if the rest of his body remains uncovered, when he finds nothing else. He should not do this deliberately while he has something else at hand; rather he wears what he finds. The worshipper drapes himself in a robe, a gown, or a cloak, laying its middle length across his shoulders, casting the end on his left over to his right and the end on his right over it to his left.

They forbade the wrapping known as ishtimal al-samma in prayer, which is that a man gathers the edge of the garment while it lies on his shoulder, draws it with his left hand to his hands, throws what was on his right over to his left, and winds himself in it as the people of the wilderness do, keeping his hands inside it. He should not pray wrapped in such a way. They permitted praying in the covering burnus, and they forbade sadl in prayer, saying it is the practice of the Jews. Sadl is to lay the robe from its middle length upon the shoulders and let its two sides hang loose beside the hands, without folding any of it back over the shoulders.
