They permitted a worshipper to pray girded with a sword, and they commended this for the Friday prayer, the two Eids, for Jihad and Ribat, and wherever a worshipper fears for himself in travel or otherwise. They saw no harm in praying in leather socks, and they permitted praying in sandals if they do not distract the worshipper and he can manage in them. They forbade praying in the garments of the polytheists that they had worn until such garments are purified. They commended that a woman pray wearing her ornaments if she has them, and that she not strip herself of this, even to the extent of tying about her neck a cord or some small thing by which she is distinguished from men; and they disliked that women divest themselves of ornament and of henna, or alter their palms, or resemble men in appearance. Everything permissible to wear is permissible to prostrate upon, whether of the growth of the earth or of other materials such as wool, hair, fur, and the like. The worshipper should stand firm in his prayer and not prostrate upon any foodstuff. They disliked that he prostrate upon any part of his own clothing, neither upon his sleeve nor upon the coil of his turban, and held that he must uncover his forehead so that it, or such part of it as he is able, reaches what he prostrates upon, together with his nose.

Concerning the Friday prayer, the Messenger of God (ص) said that four have what preceded them forgiven: the sick man when he recovers, the polytheist when he embraces Islam, the pilgrim when he completes his Hajj, and the one who returns from Jumuah in faith and in hope of reward. He said: "Pray abundantly upon me on Friday, for it is a day on which deeds are multiplied." And he said: "When Friday night comes, God Almighty sends down angels bearing pens of gold and silver and scrolls, and when the dawn breaks they record nothing but the prayers upon me and upon my Ahl al-Bayt until the sun sets. And angels stand at the doors of the mosques recording those who enter, each in his rank, first and then next, from the breaking of dawn until the Imam ascends the pulpit; when the Imam ascends the pulpit they fold their scrolls and enter to listen to the remembrance." He said: "There are three things which, if my nation knew the merit they hold, they would cast lots for them: the call to prayer, going early to Jumuah, and the first row in prayer." And he said: "Whoever goes early to the mosque on Friday and recites Surah Al Imran to its completion, seventy angels ascend with it and seventy angels receive it, and he is forgiven what lies between the two Fridays. And if he recites Surah al-Kahf, seventy angels ascend with it and seventy angels receive it, and he is gathered on the Day of Resurrection with a light spanning the distance between Mecca and Medina."

Ali said, concerning the words of God Almighty, "حَافِظُوا عَلَى الصَّلَوَاتِ وَالصَّلَوَاتِ الْوُسْطَى" ("Guard strictly the prayers and the middle prayer"), that the middle prayer is the Friday prayer, which on the remaining days is the noon prayer; and he used to command that the time of the sun's decline be carefully observed. He said: "Beware of forsaking attendance at the mosques, for one of you is soon to begin so that he comes to the mosque only on Friday, then falls back and comes one Friday and misses another, then falls back until he no longer comes at all, and so God sets a seal upon his heart." Abu Jafar Muhammad ibn Ali ibn al-Hussein (عَلَيْهِمُ السَّلَامُ) said: "Deeds are multiplied on Friday, so I love to increase my prayers and charity on that day." And Ali said: "Friday night is a resplendent night and its day is radiant. Whoever dies on Friday night, God writes for him a release from the torment of the grave; and whoever dies on Friday, God frees him from the Fire. Hell is kindled every day except Friday, for on it it is not kindled." And there is no harm in praying throughout the whole of Friday.

Ali said that the Friday prayer is an obligation, and gathering to it with the just Imam is an obligation; if a man forsakes it without excuse three times, he has forsaken three obligatory duties, and none forsakes three without excuse but a hypocrite. Jafar ibn Muhammad said that when Friday night comes, God commands an angel who calls out from the first of the night to its last, as He calls out every other night in the final third: "Is there one who asks, that I may give him? Is there one who repents, that I may turn to him? Is there one who seeks forgiveness, that I may forgive him? O seeker of good, draw near; O seeker of evil, hold back." And when the morning of Friday comes, God apportions the provisions among His servants until the sun rises. We have narrated from the Ahl al-Bayt that they commended ghusl on Friday, and disliked taking medicine on Friday and Thursday lest it weaken the one who takes it from attending Jumuah; and they enjoined perfume and the finest clothing a person can find when he intends to go out to witness the Friday prayer. And the Messenger of God (ص) said that on Friday there is an hour in which no believing servant asks God for any good but that He grants it. Ali, Abu Jafar, and Abu Abdullah (عَلَيْهِمُ السَّلَامُ) said that it is from the sun's decline until an hour has passed.

They said that Jumuah is obligatory except upon the traveler, the woman, the child, the sick, and the owned slave, and whoever has an excuse; and whoever of these attends it, it suffices him in place of the noon prayer. They said that whoever is within two farsakhs of the congregational mosque, or less, is obliged to come to Jumuah; the farsakh is three miles, and the mile is three thousand cubits. If there are three miles between two congregational mosques, then Jumuah is obligatory in each of them, even within a single town. They said that the least by which Jumuah is established is five men, one of them the Imam; if they are fewer than five they do not gather for it but pray the noon prayer, for the Friday prayer with the Imam is equal to seventy prayers. This applies when they are in a settled town.

> فَإِذَا قُضِيَتِ ٱلصَّلَوٰةُ فَٱنتَشِرُوا۟ فِى ٱلْأَرْضِ وَٱبْتَغُوا۟ مِن فَضْلِ ٱللَّهِ

Regarding the words of God Almighty, "يَا أَيُّهَا الَّذِينَ آمَنُوا إِذَا نُودِيَ لِلصَّلَاةِ مِن يَوْمِ الْجُمُعَةِ فَاسْعَوْا إِلَىٰ ذِكْرِ اللَّهِ" ("O you who believe, when the call to prayer is made on Friday, hasten to the remembrance of God") [al-Jumuah: 9], they said this is a command of God, and the hastening is neither a trot nor a run; rather the merit lies in going early before the call. When the call to prayer is given, one may not stay behind, and the one walking to it walks, catching of it what he catches, without running or trotting. And regarding His saying, "فَإِذَا قُضِيَتِ الصَّلَاةُ فَانتَشِرُوا فِي الْأَرْضِ وَابْتَغُوا مِن فَضْلِ اللَّهِ" ("And when the prayer is concluded, disperse through the land and seek the bounty of God") [al-Jumuah: 10], this is a command of permission: as He commanded hastening at the call, He permitted departure after the prayer for whoever wishes. Whoever remains in the mosque, praying, listening to the remembrance, and sitting, has the merit and reward of that; and whoever leaves after the prayer is concluded, there is no blame upon him, for God has permitted it to him.

If one enters the mosque on Friday before the prayer, he prays voluntary prayers as he wishes before the Friday prayer, and he may pray voluntary prayers after it as he wishes. They disliked that one who comes when the people have already filled the mosque should climb over their necks. Ali said: "That I should sit out Jumuah is dearer to me than that I should hold back until, when the Imam has sat, I come stepping over the necks of the people." Whoever comes to the mosque when it is full should sit wherever he finds room, without stepping over the necks of the people; but if he finds them spread out so that he can walk between them to a space he sees at the front of the mosque, there is no harm in walking to it. They forbade speech once the Imam has ascended the pulpit, and enjoined the people to face him and to listen attentively to the sermon; when the Imam has finished, there is no harm in speech between that and the raising of the prayer.

When the Imam enters the mosque on Friday, he begins by ascending the pulpit, settles, turns his face to the people, greets them, and sits. The muezzins rise and give the call before him, and when they finish the adhan they sit. He rises, praises God and extols Him, prays upon the Prophet and his family, and exhorts the people as much as he is able and as is prepared for him; then he sits a brief sitting, rises, praises God again and extols Him, prays upon the Prophet and his family, and supplicates for the Imam and the believers as much as he is able. When he has finished, the muezzins rise and call the iqama; the Imam descends and leads the Friday prayer of two rakahs. In the first rakah, after the opening chapter of the Book, he recites Surah al-Jumuah, and in the second, after the opening chapter, Surah al-Munafiqun; he recites aloud, makes qunut after finishing the recitation in the second rakah, performs the tashahhud, gives the salam, and departs. He should deliver the sermon only while turbaned and girded with a sword, when that is possible and available to him. Whoever catches the last rakah of the Friday prayer with the Imam before the Imam raises his head from bowing has caught that rakah, and he adds a second rakah to it; but if he does not reach it until the Imam has raised his head, he has not caught Jumuah and prays four. If the people miss Jumuah they do not pray it; they pray the noon prayer, and if the Imam is absent and none is found to lead them in the congregational mosque, one of them leads them in the noon prayer of four rakahs.

> وَأَذَنٌۭ مِّنَ ٱللَّهِ وَرَسُولِهِۦٓ إِلَى ٱلنَّاسِ يَوْمَ ٱلْحَجِّ ٱلْأَكْبَرِ

As for the prayers of the two Eids, God Almighty said: "شَهْرُ رَمَضَانَ الَّذِي أُنزِلَ فِيهِ الْقُرْآنُ" ("The month of Ramadan in which the Quran was sent down"), through to His saying: "وَلِتُكْمِلُوا الْعِدَّةَ وَلِتُكَبِّرُوا اللَّهَ عَلَىٰ مَا هَدَاكُمْ وَلَعَلَّكُمْ تَشْكُرُونَ" ("that you may complete the number and magnify God for having guided you, and that you may give thanks") [al-Baqarah: 185]. And He said: "وَأَذَانٌ مِنَ اللَّهِ وَرَسُولِهِ إِلَى النَّاسِ يَوْمَ الْحَجِّ الْأَكْبَرِ" ("And a proclamation from God and His Messenger to the people on the day of the Greater Pilgrimage") [al-Tawbah: 3]. We have narrated from the Imams of the household of the Messenger of God ﷺ that the day of the Greater Pilgrimage is the day of sacrifice, and that within it fall the Visitation, the Tawaf al-Ifadah, the slaughter, the shaving, the casting of the pebbles. They said that the takbeer is after the completion of the number of the fast, on the night of Fitr and the day of Fitr, and likewise the takbeer is made on Adha, and we shall mention that in its place, God willing.

Ali said: "It pleases me that a person free himself four nights in the year for prayer and the remembrance of God: the night of Fitr, the night of Adha, the night of the middle of Shaban, and the first night of Rajab." And Ali said: "I heard the Messenger of God (ص) address the people on the day of Adha, saying: This is the day of thajj and ajj. As for thajj, it is the blood you shed; whoever's intention is sincere, the first drop of it is an expiation for him of every sin. And as for ajj, it is supplication, so raise your voices to God. By Him in whose hand is the soul of Muhammad, none departs from this standing place but is forgiven, save the one who persists in one of the major sins and does not resolve within himself to abandon it."

We have narrated from the Ahl al-Bayt that they commended ghusl on the day of Fitr and the day of Adha before going out to the prayer ground; that one break his fast on the day of Fitr, with whatever is available, before going out to the prayer ground, and not eat on the day of sacrifice until he returns from the prayer; that the one going out to the two Eids wear the finest clothing he has and perfume himself with what perfume is at hand; and that the Imam be turbaned, whether in winter or summer. Abu Jafar Muhammad ibn Ali, when he wished to go out to the two Eids or to Jumuah, would say: "O God, whoever has prepared and made ready and equipped and provided himself to arrive before a creature, hoping for its bounty and its gift, then to You, O God, is my preparation, my making ready, my equipping, and my provisioning, hoping for Your bounty, Your gift, and Your favors. I have gone out to You, presenting no righteous deed I have advanced and no intercession of any creature in whom I have placed hope, save the intercession of Muhammad and his family. Rather I have gone out to You confessing my sins and my wrongdoing against myself. O Magnificent, O Magnificent, O Magnificent, forgive the great sin, for none forgives the great sin but You. O Exalted, O Magnificent, there is no god but You." And they related the showing of weapons, equipment, and mounts on the day of Eid, that one may take pride in it and awe the enemy by it. The pulpit is brought out and set up on open ground; and if that is near the burial-ground, the cemetery, that is better. And the Imam goes out.

The Commander of the Believers (ع) used to go out to the two Eids in Kufa proclaiming the takbeer, and around him and before him a thousand of the city's elders and men of mature years proclaimed it with him. So too the people go out in their finest garments, with dignity, magnifying Allah, declaring His oneness, and praising Him as they go and as they return. When they reach the prayer ground, they sit without offering any voluntary prayer before the Eid prayer or after it. They sit in awe and reverence, remembering Allah, not pressing upon one another; each man sits where he has arrived. The rows are to be spaced so that every worshipper has room to prostrate and to bow, for whoever cannot manage that has no valid prayer.

When the imam arrives he begins with the prayer before the sermon, for such is the Sunnah of the Eid, and upon this was the Messenger of Allah ﷺ and those who came after him, until Uthman changed it. He had prayed with the people at an Eid, and when he finished the prayer most of them dispersed and did not stay to hear the sermon; so thereafter he set the prayer after the sermon, that they might not leave him as they leave on Friday. But it is not fitting that the Sunnah of the Messenger of Allah ﷺ be altered, or exchanged, or turned from that upon which he established it. Allah, mighty and exalted, has said: ﴿وَمَا آتَاكُمُ الرَّسُولُ فَخُذُوهُ وَمَا نَهَاكُمْ عَنْهُ فَانتَهُوا﴾ [al-Hashr: 7]. And He said: ﴿فَلْيَحْذَرِ الَّذِينَ يُخَالِفُونَ عَنْ أَمْرِهِ أَن تُصِيبَهُمْ فِتْنَةٌ أَوْ يُصِيبَهُمْ عَذَابٌ أَلِيمٌ﴾ [al-Nur: 63].

> لَا يُكَلِّفُ ٱللَّهُ نَفْسًا إِلَّا وُسْعَهَا

The imam opens the prayer without adhan and without iqama, for there is neither adhan nor iqama for the two Eids. He pronounces the takbir al-ihram, then recites the Opening of the Book and Surah al-Shams wa duhaha, reciting aloud. Then he pronounces five takbeers, and between each two of them he offers a light qunut, which is to say: "O Allah, forgive me, have mercy upon me, grant me well-being, and pardon me in this world and the hereafter; You are powerful over all things." Then he pronounces the sixth takbir, rising with it, and prostrates two prostrations. He stands for the second rakah and recites the Opening of the Book and Surah Hal ataka hadith al-Ghashiyah. Then he pronounces four takbeers, and between each two of them a light qunut, then the fifth takbir, rising with it, and prostrates two prostrations. Then he gives the tashahhud and the salam.

He departs and ascends the pulpit, and the people fall silent, facing him, seated in their places, and he delivers two sermons as we described for the Friday prayer, save that in the sermon of Eid al-Fitr he mentions the Sunnah of the Fitr charity and enjoins it, and in the sermon of Eid al-Adha he mentions the Sunnah of the sacrifice and urges it upon whoever is able and possesses the means. Then he descends and departs, and the people depart. It is recommended that they return by a route other than the one on which they went out, if they find this possible and are able. Whoever misses the Eid prayer with the imam prays it alone in his house as four rakahs. Women are permitted to go out to the two Eids. Upon the people of the open country there is no obligation of gathering or of going out for the two Eids; the Eid is held only in the settled towns, wherever the Friday prayer is gathered. The Eid prayer is established by the same number that establishes the Friday prayer, five men one of whom is the imam; if they are fewer than that, no Eid is held for them. There is no Eid upon the traveler, though if the people of the wilderness attend, and any of the travelers choose to attend the Eid, that is good and there is merit in it for them. It is fitting that one of the people of the wilderness who is within two leagues of the town or nearer should witness the Eid.

If the day of Eid falls upon a Friday, they pray the Eid at its time and the Friday at its time, and the people of the wilderness return to their dwellings if these are distant, once the Eid prayer is done, which they prefer. If the people rise on the day of Eid not knowing of it, and then before midday it becomes clear to them that it is the Eid, they go out and pray the Eid and break their fast if it is Eid al-Fitr. But if they do not know of it until after midday, they break their fast and then go out the following day, at the hour of going out to the Eid prayer, and make up the Eid prayer just as they would have prayed it on the day itself. The time of going out to the two Eids is when the sun has risen somewhat, and they delay going out on the day of Fitr a little later than on the day of Adha.

The takbeer of the days of Tashreeq runs from the Fajr prayer on the day of Arafah to the Asr prayer on the last of the days of Tashreeq; after that Asr the takbeer is pronounced and then cut off. The takbeer of the days of Tashreeq is obligatory upon men and women, in travel and in residence, after every prescribed prayer, and there is no takbeer following a voluntary prayer. The takbeer of the days of Tashreeq is: "Allahu akbar, Allahu akbar, la ilaha illa Allah, Allahu akbar, Allahu akbar, wa lillahi al-hamd. Allahu akbar ala ma hadana, wa Allahu akbar ala ma razaqana min bahaim al-anam." So too is the takbeer while going out to the prayer ground on the day of sacrifice, and on the day of Fitr likewise, except that on it one does not say "for what He has provided us of the grazing beasts." The days of Tashreeq are the three days after the day of sacrifice, named so because in them the people spread the strips of meat, that is, they lay them out for the sun. The day of Arafah is the day before the sacrifice. The day of sacrifice is the day of Eid, following the day of Arafah. The day of settlement follows the day of sacrifice, named so because the people settle upon it at Mina. Then follows the first day of departure, then the last day of departure, which is the last of the days of Tashreeq; on it the takbeer is pronounced after the Asr prayer and then cut off. When the people pray in congregation during the days of Tashreeq, the imam gives the salam and pronounces the takbeer; when he has finished it, those behind him pronounce it, and he raises his voice and they raise theirs with the takbeer. Whoever the imam has preceded in part of the prayer, once the imam has given the salam, rises and makes up what he missed, then pronounces the takbeer after he has given the salam.

As for forgetfulness in the prayer: it is related from the Imams of the House of the Messenger of Allah ﷺ that they said: whoever forgets the takbir al-ihram, the opening takbeer, and prays, then learns that he did not pronounce it at the beginning of his prayer, repeats the prayer. Whoever doubts whether he has bowed a rakah of his prayer bows, then prostrates the two prostrations of forgetfulness. Whoever doubts and does not know whether he has prayed one rakah or two: if it is established that he prayed one, he builds upon it and prostrates the two prostrations of forgetfulness; and if that is not established, he begins the prayer afresh, making the rakah he is in the first of his prayer, then prostrates the two prostrations of forgetfulness. If he doubts and does not know whether he has prayed two or three, he builds upon certainty, which is two, and prostrates the two prostrations of forgetfulness. If he doubts and does not know whether he has prayed three or four, he gives the salam and prays two rakahs seated and prostrates the two prostrations of forgetfulness; for if he had already prayed four, the two are a voluntary prayer for him, and if he had prayed only three, the two rakahs from sitting stand in the place of one rakah. Thus it has come from the Messenger of Allah ﷺ, that he said: the prayer of the seated, without excuse, is half the prayer of the standing. If he doubts and does not know whether he has prayed two or four, he gives the tashahhud and the salam, then rises and prays two rakahs, reciting in them the Opening of the Book, gives the tashahhud and the salam, and prostrates the two prostrations of forgetfulness; for if he had prayed only two, his prayer is complete, and if he had prayed four, his last two rakahs are a voluntary prayer for him, though he should fix his heart upon them as being of the obligation.

Whoever forgets the bowing repeats the prayer. Whoever forgets a prostration, when he remembers, gives the tashahhud and prostrates the two prostrations of forgetfulness. Whoever forgets the tashahhud prostrates the two prostrations of forgetfulness and gives the tashahhud after them and the salam. If he departs and forgets to give the salam, having given the tashahhud, then the salam of the tashahhud suffices him, meaning his words "peace be upon us and upon the righteous servants of Allah." Whoever forgets the recitation but has completed the bowing and the prostration owes no repetition; he prostrates the two prostrations of forgetfulness. But if he leaves the recitation deliberately, he repeats the prayer. If he forgets to sit for the first tashahhud and rises for the third rakah without sitting, then remembers before he bows, he sits, and prostrates the two prostrations of forgetfulness after he has finished the prayer. In all that we have mentioned, the two prostrations of forgetfulness come after the salam. If he remembers before rising and before speaking, he prostrates the two prostrations of forgetfulness, then gives a light tashahhud, gives the salam, and departs. If he has forgotten and given the salam after two rakahs, then realizes this before he has left his prayer, he completes the prayer, gives the tashahhud, and prostrates the two prostrations of forgetfulness. Likewise, if he has left the prayer and spoken, he need only bring what remained upon him and prostrate the two prostrations of forgetfulness.

So too did the Messenger of Allah ﷺ do. He forgot and gave the salam after two rakahs of a prayer he had shortened, and departed. Dhu al-Yadayn said to him: "Has the prayer been shortened, or have you forgotten, O Messenger of Allah?" He said, "How is that?" He said, "You departed after two rakahs." So he rose to the people and asked, "Is what Dhu al-Yadayn said true?" They said, "Yes, O Messenger of Allah." Then he prayed two rakahs and gave the salam, then prostrated the two prostrations of forgetfulness, then gave the salam and departed.

Whoever forgets and adds to his prayer: if he had given the tashahhud at the end of his prayer and then rose and added, his prayer is complete and what he added is a voluntary prayer, and he prostrates the two prostrations of forgetfulness. If he had not sat for the tashahhud, and the addition was a bowing, he repeats the prayer; but if the addition was only a prostration, he completes the prayer and prostrates the two prostrations of forgetfulness. Whoever forgets and does not know whether he added to his prayer or fell short, his mind inclining to nothing in particular, gives the tashahhud and the salam, then prays two rakahs reciting in them the Opening of the Book, gives the tashahhud and the salam, and prostrates the two prostrations of forgetfulness. Whoever doubts about the bowing after he has prostrated proceeds with his prayer; likewise if he doubts about the prostration after he has risen, he proceeds and owes nothing until he is certain; and likewise if he doubts about anything of his prayer after he has left it, he owes nothing until he is certain. Whoever neglects his prayer and abandons it deliberately prays it when he remembers. Whoever is often afflicted with forgetfulness proceeds upon his certainty and builds upon it and prostrates the two prostrations of forgetfulness. Whoever imagines within his prayer that he has broken his ablution proceeds with the prayer unless he is certain of it. Whoever forgets behind the imam while following him owes nothing.

As for what cuts off the prayer: whoever is met by something he fears for himself, his property, or another, or by some sudden matter that does not permit him to complete his prayer while he is within it, there is no harm in his cutting it off, then beginning and resuming the prayer. The breaking of ablution within the prayer cuts off the prayer; so when the worshipper breaks his ablution he leaves, performs the ablution, and resumes the prayer. Whoever vomits or has a nosebleed in the prayer, and is able to rinse away the vomit and wash off the blood without turning his face from the qibla, does so and builds upon his prayer; but if he turns his face from the qibla, he begins the prayer afresh. Whoever speaks in the prayer has cut it off and owes its repetition. Whoever, while in the prayer, remembers that he is in a state of major impurity or otherwise without purity, cuts it off, purifies himself, and begins the prayer afresh; and if he remembers this after he has prayed, he purifies himself and repeats the prayer, and if he was an imam, all who prayed by his prayer repeat theirs. The worshipper does not cut off the prayer on account of something passing before him, but wards it off as much as he can. Ali said: the Messenger of Allah ﷺ prayed one day, and there passed before him a woman, then a donkey passed, then a dog passed, while we were watching. When he had finished he said, "I have seen what you have seen; nothing cuts off the prayer of the believer. But ward off what you are able to ward off."

As for one who has been preceded in part of the prayer: when a man arrives while the imam is leading the people, and enters into their prayer, he pronounces the takbeer of consecration. If he reaches the imam before he bows, or before he raises his head from the bowing, he bows with him and counts that rakah. If he does not reach him until he has raised his head from the bowing, he enters with him in his prayer and prostrates with his prostration but does not count that rakah; he counts what comes after it and takes that as the beginning of his prayer. If the imam has preceded him by a rakah, he prays the second with him; and when the imam sits for the first tashahhud, the latecomer does not sit fully settled but sits poised to rise, and does not give the tashahhud. When the imam rises for the third rakah, it is the latecomer's second. When the imam prostrates, he prostrates with him; and when the imam rises for the third, the latecomer returns briefly to the ground, gives a light tashahhud, then rises. When the imam sits, the latecomer sits as he sat at first, poised, and does not give the tashahhud; and when the imam gives the salam, the latecomer does not give it but rises and makes up a rakah, then sits settled, gives the tashahhud and the salam, and departs.

If the imam has preceded him by two rakahs, he takes the one he caught with him as the first of his prayer; and when the imam gives the salam, the latecomer does not give it but rises and brings two rakahs to complete his prayer, then gives the tashahhud and the salam and departs. The tashahhud he performs at the imam's final one is, for him, the first tashahhud. So too, if he catches only a single rakah of the prayer, he builds upon it and makes it for himself the first of his prayer, observing at the imam's sitting and rising the like of what we have described for one who has missed a rakah. If it is the Maghrib prayer and the imam has preceded him by a rakah, he enters with him in the second, likewise taking it as the first of his prayer, and sits when the imam sits for the first tashahhud, poised as we described, without giving the tashahhud. When the imam rises for the second rakah, the latecomer takes it as his own second; and when the imam sits for the last tashahhud, the latecomer sits with him and gives, for himself, the first tashahhud. When the imam gives the salam, the latecomer rises and prays one rakah, sits, gives the tashahhud and the salam, and departs. If he does not catch the rakah with him, he takes what he has as the first of his prayer, and when the imam sits to give the tashahhud he sits as we described, poised, without giving the tashahhud; and when the imam gives the salam, he rises and prays a rakah reciting in it the Opening of the Book and a surah, then sits for the first tashahhud, then rises and completes his prayer. And if the imam has preceded him by a rakah in the Fajr prayer, and the imam sits for the tashahhud, the latecomer sits with him as we described, poised, without giving the tashahhud; and when the imam gives the salam, the latecomer rises and prays a rakah, then sits, gives the tashahhud and the salam, and departs. If the latecomer forgets and gives the salam with the imam's salam, then remembers, he makes up what he missed and prostrates the two prostrations of forgetfulness. This is the teaching of the Imams of the House of the Messenger of Allah ﷺ.

If the imam has preceded a latecomer by one rakah in the Fajr prayer, and the imam sits for the testimony, the latecomer sits as well in the manner already described, poised and ready, but does not recite the testimony himself. When the imam gives the salutation, the latecomer rises and prays a single rakah, then sits, recites the testimony, gives the salutation, and departs. Should the latecomer forget that the imam had already given the salutation and give it with him, then remember, he makes up what he missed and performs the two prostrations of omission. This is the teaching of the imams of the household of the Messenger of Allah ﷺ.

As for the time at which boys are commanded to pray, the Messenger of Allah ﷺ said: "The pen is lifted from three: from the child until he reaches puberty, from the sleeper until he wakes, and from the insane until he recovers." In this there is proof that prayer does not fall upon boys as a binding obligation until they reach puberty. Yet it has come down from Ali and the imams of his lineage that they directed boys to be taken to prayer once they can understand it, and to fasting once they can bear it, before puberty, not as a strict duty but so that they may be trained in it, grow accustomed to it, and settle into it. Then, when they arrive at the age of puberty, they will already know it, will have been keeping it, and will not be plunged into it all at once in a way that breeds weariness, restlessness, and indifference. For when habits are laid down early, they become like a second nature.

It has likewise come from them that whoever takes up a good deed, whether prayer or fasting or any of the other gates of righteousness, and persists in it, should hold to it without cutting it off before a full year has passed over it. By this they intended to lead people to good by degrees and to make it light upon them. Had they said, "persist in it forever," the weight of it would have fallen heavy on those who heard. But when a man is told, "keep to this for a year," it is easier for him, and once he has kept to it a year it becomes for him a nature and a habit he cannot cut off, for he has grown familiar with it, found ease in it, and taken comfort in it, so that it is hardly possible for him to abandon it. In the same spirit they commanded that boys be taken to prayer and fasting before puberty. They said: children are to be commanded to pray at seven years of age, and disciplined for neglecting it at nine. They are to be separated in their beds at ten, and this separation at that age is a precaution, since their nearness to one another in sleep coincides with their nearness to puberty and the first stirring of desire, lest their closeness in bed lead to anything to be feared. This is the discipline of the friends of Allah toward His servants, whom Allah has entrusted to their care and whose affairs of religion and of the world He has committed to their keeping.

> فَسِيحُوا۟ فِى ٱلْأَرْضِ أَرْبَعَةَ أَشْهُرٍۢ

As for the traveler's prayer, the Messenger of Allah ﷺ said: "Allah, blessed and exalted, has bestowed on my nation a gift He has bestowed on no other nation before it," an act of honor from Allah, mighty and majestic. It was said: "O Messenger of Allah, what is this gift?" He said: "The breaking of the fast and the shortening of the prayers in travel. Whoever does not do so has returned to Allah His gift." Ali said: "Whoever shortens the prayer in travel has accepted the relief of Allah, mighty and majestic, and his prayer is complete." And it was said that the Messenger of Allah ﷺ forbade that the prayer be prayed in full during travel. Jafar ibn Muhammad said: "I am innocent of anyone who prays four rakahs in travel." Abu Jafar Muhammad ibn Ali said that whoever prays the prayer in full while traveling, knowing that the sunnah is to shorten it, must repeat the prayer, but whoever does not know this need not repeat it.

They said that shortening in travel means to pray every prayer as two rakahs, except Maghrib, which is not shortened in travel or at home. They said that the traveler prays the sunnah and the supererogatory prayers as well; if he is journeying by day and the road presses him on, he prays the obligatory prayer as two rakahs and delays the last of the sunnah until he alights at night, when he makes it up together with the night prayer. This they held praiseworthy and did not approve its neglect. The traveler does not pray in full, nor does he pray fewer than the full number in the case of any obligatory prayer, whether in travel or at home.

They said that the distance of travel at which shortening and breaking the fast become obligatory is two barids, twenty-four thousand paces. The barid is four farsakhs, the farsakh is three miles, and the mile is three thousand cubits, which comes to one thousand moderate paces measured by a single leg, from where the right foot is lifted to where it is set down. Even if the place a traveler intends is but a single barid away, and he has there neither family nor dwelling, and intends to return from it within less than ten days, he shortens and breaks his fast until he departs. The traveler breaks his fast and shortens his prayers from the moment he leaves his house and sets out on his journey, until he returns and enters his house, provided he is pressing on in his travel.

If a traveler enters a congregational prayer led by residents, he gives the salutation after two rakahs and departs. If a traveler leads residents in prayer, he ends at two rakahs and then bids them complete the prayer. Both of these are disliked, as has been mentioned, because the ruling of the traveler in prayer is not the ruling of the resident. Whoever has an unpaid prayer from his time of residence and remembers it while traveling makes it up in full as it was owed. Whoever has an unpaid prayer from his travel and remembers it while resident makes it up according to how it was, shortened.

The traveler may pray voluntary prayers while mounted, wherever he turns, toward the Qibla or away from it. This rests upon the word of Allah, mighty and majestic: ﴿فَأَيْنَمَا تُوَلُّوا فَثَمَّ وَجْهُ اللَّهِ﴾ "Wherever you turn, there is the face of Allah" [al-Baqarah: 115]. The Messenger of Allah ﷺ used to pray voluntary prayers upon his mount on his return from the Farewell Pilgrimage, with the House behind his back. But this is permitted only in the supererogatory prayer; the obligatory prayer is not prayed except upon the ground, turned toward the Qibla. They forbade praying upon the beaten track, that is, the highway where people pass, or in the space between two roads.

At sea the traveler shortens the prayer and prays aboard the ship. If the ship turns, he turns his face with it toward the Qibla; if he cannot pray standing, he prays seated. One who is drowning, one who is naked, and one wading through water pray by gesture, making the prostration lower than the bowing, and the naked person likewise prays seated when he finds nothing to cover his nakedness.

Shortening is not for one who goes out merely to take his ease, nor for a governor making his rounds among his people, nor for a tax-gatherer moving about in his collection, nor for the hired driver, nor for those seafarers whose very calling is perpetual travel, nor for one who alights in his travel upon a household of his own intending to stay with them. Whoever alights at a place in his travel intending to remain ten days prays in full; if he intends less, he shortens. If his stay is drawn out without such intention, while he intends each day to depart, he shortens for as long as a month, and after the month he prays in full until he leaves.

As for the prayer of the sick, we have it from the Ahl al-Bayt that if the sick person cannot pray standing, he prays lying on his back with the Qibla before his face in the direction of his feet, or lying on his right side with his face toward the Qibla, gesturing so that his prostration is lower than his bowing. Likewise, one who prays seated and cannot bow and prostrate does the same. One afflicted with a nosebleed that will not stop, or a wound that keeps discharging and cannot be bound and cannot be held back, performs ablution and prays in that state, for Allah is most ready to excuse His servant when He has tried him.

If the sick person cannot pray anything beyond the obligatory, then when he recovers he may, if he wishes, make up the sunnah and supererogatory prayers he left, and in that there is merit and reward, though their making-up is not binding as the making-up of the obligatory is binding. If he can pray seated with his legs folded, he folds them as he would fold them in sitting during the standing; if he cannot, he prays however he is able and it suffices him. It suffices him to recite the Opening of the Book alone, and if he can do no other, to say a single glorification in his bowing and his prostration when he can manage no more. If he is able to prostrate, he prostrates; if not, he nods with his head, making the prostration lower than the bowing; and if he cannot nod his head, he signals with what he can, his head or his eye, as he is able, for Allah does not burden a soul beyond its capacity. If he is able to pray standing, he prays only so, unless standing strengthens his illness and increases it, in which case he may pray in the manner described according to what he can do. Whoever falls into a swoon makes up the prayers he missed, be they few or many, when he comes to.

As for the prayer of fear, Allah, mighty and majestic, said: ﴿فَلَيْسَ عَلَيْكُمْ جُنَاحٌ أَن تَقْصُرُوا مِنَ الصَّلَاةِ إِنْ خِفْتُمْ أَن يَفْتِنَكُمُ الَّذِينَ كَفَرُوا إِنَّ الْكَافِرِينَ كَانُوا لَكُمْ عَدُوًّا مُّبِينًا وَإِذَا كُنتَ فِيهِمْ فَأَقَمْتَ لَهُمُ الصَّلَاةَ فَلْتَقُمْ طَائِفَةٌ مِّنْهُم مَّعَكَ﴾ "There is no blame upon you if you shorten the prayer, if you fear that those who disbelieve may harm you. Indeed, the disbelievers are to you a clear enemy. And when you are among them and lead them in prayer, let a party of them stand with you" [al-Nisa: 101 to 102], down to His words: "a humiliating punishment." Thus Allah, mighty and majestic, granted the relief of shortening the prayer in the state of fear, making no exception in it of travel from residence. Abu Abd-Allah Jafar ibn Muhammad ibn Ali was asked about the prayer of fear and the prayer of travel, whether both are shortened, and he said: "Yes, and the prayer of fear is more deserving of shortening than the prayer of travel, for in the prayer of travel there is no fear."

We have it from the imams of the Ahl al-Bayt that the Messenger of Allah ﷺ prayed the prayer of fear in the campaign of Dhat al-Riqa. He divided his companions into two parties, stationing one facing the enemy and placing the other behind him. He said the opening takbir and they said it, he recited and they listened, he bowed and they bowed, he prostrated and they prostrated. Then the Messenger of Allah ﷺ stood, and this party prayed a further rakah on their own, gave the salutation one to another, and withdrew to their companions, standing in their place facing the enemy. Then the other party came and stood behind the Messenger of Allah ﷺ. He said the takbir and they said it, he recited and they listened, he bowed and they bowed, he prostrated and they prostrated, and he sat and they sat and bore witness. When the Messenger of Allah ﷺ gave the salutation, they rose and made up a further rakah for themselves, bore witness, gave the salutation one to another, and withdrew.

They said: thus is the prayer of fear prayed, whenever it is possible, in all the prayers save Maghrib, which is not shortened, as was mentioned before. The first party prays one rakah with the imam and prays two rakahs for itself, according to what we have described of the prayer of the Messenger of Allah ﷺ, and the other party prays two rakahs with him, so that each party may hear the recitation in the first two rakahs, in which he recites aloud.

They said: if they cannot pray in this manner and the fear grows severe, they pray mounted upon their beasts or standing on their feet, nodding with their heads and making the prostration lower than the bowing, neither dismounting nor prostrating upon the ground when they are unable to do so. If they cannot face the Qibla, they face it at the first takbir and then turn to meet the enemy. This is the word of Allah, mighty and majestic: ﴿فَإِنْ خِفْتُمْ فَرِجَالًا أَوْ رُكْبَانًا﴾ "If you are in fear, then on foot or riding" [al-Baqarah: 239]. If the fighting closes in and there is the clash of swords and the thrust of spears so that they cannot even gesture, they pray with the takbir alone, saying a takbir for each rakah with the intention of prayer. In just this way the Commander of the Believers, Ali, and his companions prayed on the night and the day of al-Harir.

As for the eclipse prayer, Ali said that the Messenger of Allah ﷺ said: "The moon was eclipsed while Jibril (عَلَيْهِ السَّلَامُ) was with me, and I said to him: What is this, O Jibril? He said: Truly it is more obedient to Allah than you, and it has not disobeyed Allah since He created it; but this is a sign and a lesson. I said: What is to be done at such a time? He said: Prayer and the recitation of the Quran." When the sun or the moon was eclipsed, he would say to the people: "Hasten to your mosques."

We have it from the imams of the Ahl al-Bayt that the prayer for the eclipse of the sun and of the moon is one and the same, save that the prayer for the eclipse of the sun is a little longer. When an eclipse occurs, the people are to be summoned to prayer without the adhan, and they gather at the Grand Mosque if they are in a city. The imam comes forward and they draw up behind him. He says the takbir aloud and they say it, then he begins the recitation with the Opening of the Book and the longest surah he is able to recite, reciting aloud. Then he says the takbir and bows, and they bow, and he remains long in the bowing and they remain with him for as long as he recited, glorifying and saying what is said in the bowing. Then he raises his head with a takbir and they raise theirs, and he recites the Opening of the Book and a long surah like the first, and after the recitation and before the bowing he makes the qunut, then bows in the same way and rises with a takbir. So he does through five bowings, making the qunut after each pair. When he raises his head from the fifth, he says: "Allah hears the one who praises Him," saying this only there and in the tenth, at which he prostrates. In the remaining bowings he says "Allahu akbar" when he raises his head, and then prostrates.

He draws out the prostration as he drew out the bowing, glorifying and saying what is said in prostration. Then he raises his head and sits a while between the two prostrations, saying what is said between them in its fullest form, then makes the second prostration in the same way, rises, and prays a further five bowings like the first. Then he prostrates and sits for a long testimony in which he calls upon Allah, turns to Him in longing, and asks Him for what he can of this world and the Hereafter in supplication, and then gives the salutation. If the eclipse has cleared, he departs; if not, he remains seated in his place facing the Qibla and bids the people sit likewise, reciting the Quran, supplicating, and remembering Allah until it clears or passes away.

The eclipse prayer is not prayed until the eclipse is seen and made plain. No one relies in this on the word of an astrologer that the sun has been eclipsed when its eclipse has not been seen, or that says it was eclipsed by night, or the moon by day, when it is not seen. The prayer for the eclipse of the sun is prayed only by day, and the prayer for the eclipse of the moon only by night, after the eclipse has been seen. The qunut in the eclipse prayer is in every pair of bowings before the bowing, in the second, the fourth, the sixth, the eighth, and the tenth. There is no harm in reciting in each rakah, at each standing, a surah or more, apportioning the recitation across the bowings. If he does this, he recites the Opening of the Book only at the outset with the surah, making the beginning of each standing the opening of a surah, and likewise when he rises from the prostration into the second.

The eclipse prayer is not to be performed until the eclipse has actually been seen and confirmed. One should not rely on someone merely saying that the sun has eclipsed when he has not seen it himself, nor on a claim made at night that the moon has eclipsed by day, nor by day that the sun has eclipsed by night. The solar eclipse prayer is prayed only during the day, and the lunar eclipse prayer only at night, and in both cases only after the eclipse has been seen.

The qunut in this prayer is performed in every second rakah, before the second, fourth, sixth, eighth, and tenth bowings. It is also permitted to recite in every rakah, and more than that, before bowing. When one does so, the Opening of the Book is recited only at the outset of each surah, so that he makes it the first opening of the surah, and likewise when he rises from the prostration in the second rakah of the eclipse prayer.

These two rakahs, with their four prostrations and their five bowings in each rakah, were the form in which the Commander of the Faithful, Ali, led the people in the eclipse prayer. He prayed it at any time, by night or by day, whether traveling or resident in a city. If the eclipse fell at the time of an obligatory prayer, he would begin with the obligatory prayer. But if he had already entered the eclipse prayer and feared that the time of the obligatory prayer would lapse, he would interrupt the eclipse prayer, perform the obligatory prayer, and then, having finished it, resume and build upon what he had already completed of the eclipse prayer. In the same manner he prayed at every sign that occurred, whether wind, darkness, tremors, earthquakes, or any other portent, just as the eclipse prayer is prayed. Whoever misses the eclipse prayer, or the prayer of the signs, does not make it up. It is recommended that the eclipse prayer be performed in an open, cleared space so that the clearing of the eclipse may be seen. If the imam departs, those who can see the eclipse should be informed of it; and if he does not depart, the people are instructed to remain seated in their places, as we have mentioned.

> وَإِذِ ٱسْتَسْقَىٰ مُوسَىٰ لِقَوْمِهِۦ فَقُلْنَا ٱضْرِب

As for the prayer of al-Istisqa, the seeking of rain, Allah Almighty said: "And when Moses sought water for his people, We said: Strike the rock with your staff" (Al-Baqarah: 60). It is narrated that when the people of Moses (ع) were in need of water, and rain later fell upon Madinah, the Messenger of Allah went out and prayed for it, and they were so drenched that they grew concerned for themselves from the intensity of the rain. We have narrated from the Ahl al-Bayt that the prayer of al-Istisqa is like the two Eid prayers. The imam goes out, and the people go out to the prayer ground in humility before their Lord, in fear and self-abasement, and they do not carry weapons as they do for the Eid. The imam prays two rakahs without the call to prayer and without the iqamah, giving the takbeer as he gives it for the two festival prayers, and reciting in them as he recites for the two Eids. Then he ascends the pulpit, and when he reaches the top he sits briefly, then stands and turns his cloak, placing what was on his right side onto his left and what was on his left onto his right, as the Messenger of Allah did.

He begins his sermon with the takbeer, the tahleel, and the seeking of forgiveness, offering as much of this as he is able. Then he praises Allah and sends blessings upon the Prophet and his family, and he urges the people to seek forgiveness, to repent, and to do good deeds, making them aware that rain is withheld on account of their sins. He repents to Allah Almighty on their behalf, asks Him, and implores Him for water and mercy to the extent that he is able. It is recommended that this prayer be performed on a Monday.

Concerning the prayers of the Shaf, the Witr, and the two rakahs of Fajr, Allah Almighty said: "By the even and the odd" (Al-Fajr: 3), swearing by all of that. The shaf, in the speech of the Arabs, is the paired or even, and the witr is the odd. In prayer, the witr consists of three rakahs, a sunnah of the night prayer performed after the final Isha, and no prayer is prayed after it until the dawn breaks. The name witr is applied to all three rakahs; it may also refer to the single final rakah, with the two rakahs before it being the shaf, and this is what is preferred in what is established from the words of the Ahl al-Bayt. The worshipper gives the salam after the two rakahs, then stands and prays a single rakah as witr.

There has come strong encouragement toward the witr, which the Messenger of Allah commanded, saying that Allah is witr and loves the witr, so pray the witr, O people of the Quran. Ali was severe regarding it and did not permit its abandonment. Jafar ibn Muhammad said that the witr ought not to be abandoned, yet it is not among the prescribed obligatory prayers but is of the sunnah; it is recommended that whoever misses it make it up, though this is not obligatory upon him as a prescribed duty, since it is not among the obligations. Abu Jafar Muhammad ibn Ali granted a dispensation that the witr may be prayed while traveling upon a mount, as the supererogatory prayers are prayed. They preferred that the witr be prayed at the end of the night, and whoever prays it at the beginning of the night has fulfilled it.

They preferred that in its two rakahs one recite the Opening of the Book, and in the first of them "Glorify the name of your Lord, the Most High, who created" (Al-Ala (اَلْأَعْلَى)), and in the second the Opening of the Book and "Say: O disbelievers" (Al-Kafirun), and in the third "Say: He is Allah, the One" (Al-Ikhlas (اَلْإِخْلَاص)) after the Opening of the Book. In the witr he performs the qunut after bowing, raising his hands in it and supplicating with whatever comes readily to him. In the Fajr prayer he performs the qunut before bowing and after the recitation. The two rakahs of Fajr are prayed before the Fajr prayer: the call to Fajr is given, and then he prays two rakahs after the dawn has broken. It is preferred to keep them light, reciting in the first the Opening of the Book and "Say: O disbelievers," and in the second the Opening of the Book and "Say: He is Allah, the One." He then makes the tashahhud, remains seated in his place of prayer, and continues to supplicate until he prays the Fajr. They said that there is nothing in supplication that is fixed and appointed such that no other will suffice in its place, neither in the prayer nor outside it; whoever wishes to supplicate may call upon Allah Almighty with whatever he wishes.

We have narrated from the Ahl al-Bayt many forms of supplication and qunut, all of which show that there is nothing fixed and appointed in it, as we have said. The least and lightest of the qunut is that one glorify Allah within it with three glorifications. There have come from them long, short, and medium supplications for the qunut of the witr and the qunut of the Fajr. Among what has come from them of the medium kind, upon which the narrations agree, is that they instructed that one say in the qunut of the witr:

"O Allah, You see and are not seen, and You are in the highest of vantages. To You are lifted the eyes and moved the feet; toward You necks are stretched and hands spread open; by tongues You are called upon, and to You deeds are submitted. O You to whom hands are stretched out, O You to whom hearts have turned, O You before whom eyes are cast down, O You to whom necks are bowed: we complain to You of the hardship of the age, the massing of enemies, the fewness of our number, and the division of our hearts. We give thanks to You for Your blessings through our guardian and our imam, the son of the daughter of our Prophet, named the imam of his age, our guide to You and the one who points us to You. We ask You to send blessings upon him and upon his forefathers, and to support him with a victory by which You give might to Your religion and by which You aid Your friends. O Allah, gather hearts upon Your obedience and his obedience and upon holding to his imamate, and support him against his renegade enemies, O God of creation, Lord of the worlds. O Allah, make certainty firm in my heart, and increase me in light, guidance, and forgiveness, and guide me to Your straight path. Ameen, Ameen. I ask You, my Lord, for good in this world and good in the Hereafter, and I ask You to guard me from the torment of the Fire."

Whoever wishes to lengthen the supplication may add to it as he wishes. And in the qunut of the Fajr:

"O Allah, we praise You, and seek Your help, and seek Your forgiveness, and extol You with good, and give thanks to You and do not deny You; we humble ourselves to You and submit to You, and we disown whoever is wicked toward You and denies You. O Allah, You alone we worship, and to You we pray and prostrate, and to You we strive and hasten; we hope for Your mercy and fear Your severe punishment, for Your punishment surely overtakes the disbelievers. O Allah, punish the disbelievers, the hypocrites, the liars, and those who deny Your friends, the imams from the pure family of Your Prophet, and send down upon them Your scourge, Your might, Your anger, and Your torment. O Allah, punish the disbelievers among the People of the Book, and the idolaters, men and women. Forgive me, and the believing men and women and the Muslim men and women, and set right what is between them, and unite their word upon Your obedience and the obedience of Your friends, and make firm in their hearts faith and wisdom, and steady them upon the religion of Your Prophet, and support them against Your enemy and their enemy. O Allah, guide me among those You have guided, take me into Your care among those You have taken, heal me among those You have healed, bless me in what You have given, and protect me from the evil of what You have decreed, for You decree and none decrees against You. He whom You befriend is not humiliated, and he whom You oppose is not honored. Blessed are You and exalted. There is no god but You. I seek Your forgiveness and repent to You, and I ask You, my Lord, for good in this world and good in the Hereafter, and I ask You to guard me from the torment of the Fire."

And in the qunut of the Friday prayer and in the qunut of the eclipse:

"There is no god but Allah, the Wise, the Generous. There is no god but Allah, the High, the Great. Glory be to Allah, Lord of the seven heavens and Lord of the seven earths and all that is within them and between them, and Lord of the Mighty Throne. Praise be to Allah, Lord of the worlds. O Allah, send blessings upon Muhammad and upon the family of Muhammad, and make my heart firm upon Your religion and the religion of Your Prophet, and let not my heart swerve after You have guided me, and grant me mercy from Yourself, for You are the Bestower. O Allah, make me one of those You have created for Your Garden and chosen for Your religion, and turn to me in acceptance, for You are capable of all things, and You are the Accepter of repentance, the Merciful. And send blessings upon Muhammad and upon the family of Muhammad, as You are worthy of it and as they are worthy of it from You, may."

As for the obligatory prayer, it is seventeen rakahs in each day and night. The Dhuhr prayer is the first of them, called the first because it was the first prayer that the Messenger of Allah prayed, at the hour in which the obligation of prayer was sent down upon him. It is four rakahs. In the first two he recites the Opening of the Book and a surah and makes the tashahhud after them, and in the last two he recites the Opening of the Book alone, then makes the tashahhud and the tasleem. The Asr is likewise four rakahs, as Dhuhr, with the same recitation. The Maghrib is three rakahs: he makes the tashahhud after two rakahs, reciting aloud in them, then rises and prays a third rakah in which he recites the Opening of the Book silently, then sits, makes the tashahhud, and departs. The final Isha is four rakahs, like Dhuhr, except that he recites aloud in its first two rakahs. Then the Fajr is two rakahs in which he recites aloud. That is seventeen rakahs.

The sunnah prayer is the like of the obligatory prayer in number, its recitation all silent, though if one wishes he may recite a little aloud in the night prayer. He gives the salam from all of it after every two rakahs, and in each rakah he recites the Opening of the Book and a surah. These are six rakahs before Dhuhr, after the sun has passed its zenith, and four after it; four rakahs before Asr, after which there is no prayer until the sun sets; six rakahs after Maghrib before the final Isha, and four before Isha and four after it; and the witr, three rakahs, from which he gives the salam after two, then rises and prays the third, then prays two rakahs seated; and two rakahs before the Fajr prayer. That is, in number, thirty-five rakahs; but since the two rakahs seated are counted as one rakah of standing, the total is thirty-four, which is the like of the obligatory prayer. It is called the sunnah prayer because the Messenger of Allah used to pray it and bound himself to it, and likewise the imams from his progeny bound themselves to it, commanded their friends and their followers to it, encouraged them toward it, and mentioned to them its merit and its reward.

> وَمِنَ ٱلَّيْلِ فَتَهَجَّدْ بِهِۦ نَافِلَةًۭ لَّكَ عَسَىٰٓ أَن يَبْعَثَكَ رَبُّكَ مَقَامًۭا مَّحْمُودًۭا

As for the supererogatory prayer, it has no limit. It is a voluntary act; whoever wishes prays of it what he wishes at the times in which prayer is permitted, by night or by day. It is narrated of Ali ibn al-Husayn that he used to pray a thousand rakahs in each day and night when he was able, and the most excellent of this is what is prayed at night and kept vigil with after sleep. Allah Almighty said to His Prophet Muhammad: "And in a part of the night keep vigil with it, as an additional prayer for you; perhaps your Lord will raise you to a praised station" (Al-Isra: 79). Of a man it is said that he "hajada" when he sleeps, and "tahajjada" when he rises from sleep for some matter he intends; so he awakes from his sleep for it and keeps vigil with it as an additional prayer, meaning with the Quran. And He said: "O you who are wrapped in garments, rise the night except a little, half of it, or lessen from it a little" (Al-Muzzammil: 1-4), to the end of it.

> وَٱسْتَغْفِرُوا۟ ٱللَّهَ ۖ إِنَّ ٱللَّهَ غَفُورٌۭ رَّحِيمٌۢ

The Messenger of Allah used to stand at night until his feet would swell from the length of his standing, and he forbade a man to sleep the whole night through, so that he would be like a carcass thrown down. It is recommended to prolong the standing in the night prayer, and one ought not to abandon it, but rather to set for himself a fixed portion of it. Abu Jafar Muhammad ibn Ali said: I hold in contempt the servant who has read the Quran and then wakes in the night yet does not rise, until, when the morning draws near, he rises and hurries through his prayer. Whoever has not read the whole of the Quran may recite of it what comes readily to him. Allah Almighty said: "So recite what is easy for you of the Quran" (Al-Muzzammil: 20). Whoever wishes may stand the whole night with a single surah that he repeats, or with part of it, or with two surahs, or with more than that. There is no harm in reciting, in the supererogatory prayer, part of a surah in the rakah together with the Opening of the Book, or in joining two surahs within it, or many surahs. Some of them would stand the whole night, or most of it, with a single verse that he repeated whenever he came to it, a verse of promise and warning, pondering it and repeating it so that he might understand it and grow humble before it. They preferred to open the night prayer with two light rakahs and then to pray after them whatever one wishes.

> مَّآ أَفَآءَ ٱللَّهُ عَلَىٰ رَسُولِهِۦ مِنْ أَهْلِ ٱلْقُرَىٰ فَلِلَّهِ وَلِلرَّسُولِ وَلِذِى ٱلْقُرْبَىٰ

The most excellent form of the supererogatory prayer, of charity, and of all the works of righteousness is what is done in concealment and in secret; and the most excellent way to render the whole of the obligatory acts, the prayer, the zakat, and others, is openly. All the supererogatory prayers are not to be prayed in congregation, neither in the month of Ramadan nor in any other, and the people are to pray them only singly. The most excellent place for them is the houses, for in their concealment and secrecy lies their merit. The Messenger of Allah once prayed a voluntary prayer at night in the mosque during the month of Ramadan, and the people ranged themselves behind him, praying with his prayer, so he forbade them from that, entered his chamber, and prayed within it. The first to make the standing in congregation during Ramadan a practice was Umar ibn al-Khattab; yet had there been merit in it, the Messenger of Allah would not have forbidden it, and he would have been the foremost to reach it before Umar and others. The common people have narrated this very account from the Messenger of Allah, and then followed Umar in it, in opposition to the Prophet and in defiance of his prohibition. And Allah Almighty says: "Let those who go against his command beware, lest a trial befall them or a painful punishment strike them" (Al-Nur: 63), and He says: "Whatever the Messenger gives you, take it, and whatever he forbids you, abstain from it, and fear Allah" (Al-Hashr: 7). The most they have said in this matter is that the Messenger of Allah disliked it and forbade it only for those who did it with him, lest it become an obligation upon the people.

You do not claim that he granted a license to others in this matter, for had there been a license in it he would have spoken of it as a prophet, may Allah bless him and his family. When he forbade a thing, it was because Allah Almighty had made the ruling plain, and Allah had made the clear declaration an obligation upon him. So you are bound to refrain from what he forbade you, just as Allah Almighty commanded, unless a clear allowance reaches you for what you claim is permitted. They claim it was permitted, yet the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and his family, neither did it in the days of his life, nor in the days of Abu Bakr, nor in the early days of Umar. It appeared only in Umar's later period. By their own reckoning this makes it an innovation, and some of them acknowledge this while calling it a good innovation. But this contradicts the word of the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and his family: "Every innovation is misguidance, and every misguidance leads to the Fire. Follow, and do not innovate." How can an innovation be good when Allah Almighty forbade it upon the tongue of His Messenger, may Allah bless him and his family?

In the same way they invented the Duha prayer, which the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and his family, had forbidden. A man of the Ansar asked Abu Jafar Muhammad ibn Ali about it, and he answered him: "Were your own people, the Ansar, not the first to invent it?" The Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and his family, had forbidden it. They used to come from their homes and enter the mosque in the forenoon and pray, and the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and his family, forbade them from that. Then Yazid ibn Muawiyah revived it at the severed head of al-Husayn in the forenoon: he rose and prayed two rakahs and made it a fixed practice for himself, not out of anything his people held against him for what he had committed, but taking it, by his own claim, as a thanksgiving for what he had done, though it will only sink him deeper in the blaze of Hell.

Ali ibn al-Husayn held that a person should make up whatever voluntary prayers he had missed when he was kept occupied from them or overtaken by sleep. This is not an obligation, but he loved, may Allah's blessings be upon him, not to cut off or let go to waste anything that Allah Almighty had made a part of his devotion. The Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and his family, said: "Whoever intends to rise in the night, and takes to his bed, let him say: O Allah, do not leave me safe from Your cunning, and do not make me forget Your remembrance, and do not place me among the heedless. I will rise, Allah willing, at such and such an hour. Then Allah Almighty appoints for him an angel, an angel of that hour, who rouses him." And he said: "Whoever intends something of the night vigil, and sleep overcomes his eyes until morning, his sleep is a charity to him from Allah, and Allah owes him the reward of his intention and completes for him the vigil of his night."

The Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and his family, said: "Allah has an angel who, when half the night has passed, declares: Glorified and Holy is our Lord, the Lord of the angels and the Spirit, our Lord the Merciful, there is no god but He. Let those who strive arise. So whoever Allah wills arises. Then he falls silent as long as Allah wills, then he says: Glorified and Holy is our Lord, the Lord of the angels and the Spirit, our Lord the Merciful, there is no god but He. Let those who remember arise. So whoever Allah wills arises. Then when dawn breaks he says: Glorified and Holy is our Lord, the Lord of the angels and the Spirit, our Lord the Merciful, there is no god but He. Let the heedless arise."

Abu Jafar Muhammad ibn Ali said that when a third of the night has passed a caller cries out: "O seeker of good, draw near. O seeker of evil, hold back. Is there one who repents, that he may be turned to in mercy? Is there one who seeks forgiveness, that he may be forgiven? Is there one who asks, that his request may be granted? O Allah, grant every one who spends a replacement, and grant every one who withholds ruin." This call goes out every night from the passing of a third of the night until morning, and on the night of Friday from the first of the night to its last. He said: when a third of the night remains, the gates of heaven are opened, and the angel calls out in this way until morning.

The Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and his family, said: "The people of the highest ranks are set level in the Garden upon dappled horses whose bridles are of gold and whose saddles are of pearl and ruby. They pass by those who are below them, who say: Our Lord, by what have You brought Your servants to this honor? And it is said to them: They used to rise in the night while you slept, and they used to fast in the day while you ate, and they used to fight while you shrank in cowardice, and they used to spend in the way of Allah while you were miserly."

As for the mention of prostration in the Quran, Allah Almighty said: ﴿ وَإِذَا قُرِئَ عَلَيْهِمُ الْقُرْآنُ لَا يَسْجُدُونَ ﴾ [al-Inshiqaq: 21], meaning at the places of prostration within it. We have narrated from the Imams of the Ahl al-Bayt that there are fifteen prostrations in the Quran. Whoever recites them, or hears them recited, should prostrate. Some of them are obligatory, such that one is not permitted to omit the prostration at that point, and we will note these as they come.

The first prostration of the Quran is at the end of Surah al-Araf. Then in Surah al-Ra'd, at "and their shadows in the mornings and evenings." In Surah al-Nahl, at "and they do what they are commanded." In Surah al-Isra, at "and it increases them in reverence." In Surah Maryam, at "they fell down prostrate and weeping." In Surah al-Hajj, at "Allah does what He wills," and in it also at "and do good, that you may succeed." In Surah al-Furqan, at "and it increases their aversion." In Surah al-Naml, at "the Lord of the mighty Throne." In Surah al-Sajdah, at "and they are not arrogant," and this is one of the obligatory ones. In Surah Sad, at "and he bowed low and turned in repentance." In Surah Fussilat, at "if it is He whom you worship," and this is one of the obligatory ones. And at the end of Surah al-Najm, which is likewise obligatory. In Surah al-Inshiqaq, at "and when the Quran is recited to them, they do not prostrate." And at the end of Surah al-Alaq, at "Read in the name of your Lord who created," and this too is one of the obligatory ones.

Whoever is in prayer and hears a reciter reciting a verse of prostration, or has it recited before him, and does not prostrate for it, should incline his head in a nod, or he may form the intention of prostration if it is one of the obligatory ones. They disliked reciting in an obligatory prayer a surah containing one of the obligatory prostrations, for if he recites it he must prostrate, even should the verse fall at the end of the surah. In that case he prostrates, then rises and recites the Opening of the Book, then bows and prostrates. In what lies outside the obligatory prostrations he has the choice: if he wishes he prostrates, and if he wishes he does not. Whoever recites a verse of prostration while riding prostrates in whatever direction he faces, nodding with his head. There is no takbir in the prostration of the Quran, and no tashahhud, and no taslim. It is to be performed by whoever hears it, whether he was attentively listening to the Quran or reciting it himself, even if he is not in a state of ablution. He glorifies Allah while prostrate, and asks of Allah Almighty and calls upon Him with whatever he wishes.

## 5. Facing Death: The Rites of the Departed

The Book of Funerals

**Mentioning the Sick, Visiting the Ill, and Its Rewards**

We have narrated from the Messenger of Allah (ص) that Allah Almighty says: "If I afflict My servant with an illness and he is patient three times and does not complain, and Allah knows best his condition, I will replace his flesh with flesh better than his flesh and his blood with blood better than his blood. If I take him, I take him into My mercy; and if I heal him, I heal him with no sin upon him." And he (ص) said: fever is a purification from a Forgiving Lord. And he (ص) said: the groaning of the sick person, if he is patient, is written for him as good deeds; but if he panics, only panic is recorded for him and he has no reward for it.

The Commander of the Faithful, Ali ibn Abi Talib (ع), said: the sick person remains in the custody of Allah so long as he does not complain to those who visit him, and his misdeeds are wiped away from him. And he (ع) said: when Allah Almighty afflicts a servant, He drops from him of his sins in proportion to his illness; and from the fever he bears there comes to him a reward beyond counting. And he said: there is no visiting the sick until after three days, and there is no visiting of women in illness. And he said: whoever visits a sick person seeking the face of Allah and fulfilling His appointment will be in the garden of Paradise so long as he sits with the sick person; and when he departs from him, Allah sends on that day seventy thousand angels who pray for him until nightfall, and if the visit was at night, then until morning. Visiting should therefore be hastened to the early part of the day.

Allah Almighty said: ﴿فِطْرَتَ اللَّهِ الَّتِي فَطَرَ النَّاسَ عَلَيْهَا﴾ [الروم: ٣٠], the natural disposition of Allah upon which He created mankind, meaning His fashioning of them so. And he said: it is from the fitrah that the dying person, when death draws near, be turned toward the Qiblah. We have narrated from the Imams of the Ahl al-Bayt (عَلَيْهِمُ السَّلَامُ) that they commanded this, and that he be laid upon his back and set so that his face and the sole of his foot are turned toward the Qiblah, if that is possible. If it is not possible, then he is placed upon his side with his face toward the Qiblah. They also commanded that the testimony be repeated aloud at his head, "There is no god but Allah, and that Muhammad is His servant and His messenger," and that the Verse of the Throne and the two verses after it be recited at his head, then: ﴿إِنَّ رَبَّكُمُ اللَّهُ الَّذِي خَلَقَ السَّمَاوَاتِ وَالْأَرْضَ فِي سِتَّةِ أَيَّامٍ﴾ [الأعراف: ٥٤] to the end of the verse, and the last third of Surah al-Baqarah: ﴿لِلَّهِ مَا فِي السَّمَاوَاتِ وَمَا فِي الْأَرْضِ وَإِن تُبْدُوا مَا فِي أَنفُسِكُمْ أَوْ تُخْفُوهُ﴾ [البقرة: ٢٨٤] to its end. And he is supplicated for, so it is said: if his appointed term has come, make his affair easy for him and draw his soul forth from him into contentment from You and good pleasure; and give him glad tidings, and forgive him, and have mercy upon him.

Jafar ibn Muhammad (ع) said: when a believer among you dies and is prevented from speaking, the Messenger of Allah (ص) comes to him, together with Ali, Fatimah, al-Hasan, and al-Husayn. The Messenger of Allah (ص) says to him: as for what you used to fear, you have been made safe from it; and as for what you used to hope for, it is before you. Then a door to the Garden is opened for him, and he sees his dwelling within it, and it is said to him: if you wish to return to the world, you will be returned to it, and its gold and its silver are yours. He says: I have no need of the world nor of anything in it. And he recites the word of Allah Almighty: ﴿لَهُمُ الْبُشْرَىٰ فِي الْحَيَاةِ الدُّنْيَا وَفِي الْآخِرَةِ﴾ [يونس: ٦٤].

The Messenger of Allah (ص) was asked about the believer, how death may be made hard upon him and how it may be made easy for him, and likewise about the disbeliever. He said: the believing servant has a station in the Garden that he cannot reach by any measure of affliction, so death comes to him before he has reached that degree, and death is made hard upon him so that he may reach it. And it may be that Allah, Blessed and Exalted, commands the Angel of Death to draw the believer's soul out from its gentlest side, so that people see that death has been made hard upon him, though it is in truth a lightening from Allah Almighty upon His servant. Or He may command him concerning the disbeliever, so that he pulls out his soul in one wrench, like the wrenching of a roasting-spit from wet wool, so that people see that it was made easy for him, though a great punishment has already befallen him.

And he said: death is a blessing for the believer. And he (ص) said: the believer is one who finds rest and one from whom others find rest. The one who finds rest has been relieved of the grief and hardship of this world and has come to the mercy of Allah; and as for the one from whom others find rest, that is the disbeliever, from whom the servants of Allah are relieved. And he said: increase the remembrance of the destroyer of pleasures. It was asked: O Messenger of Allah, what is the destroyer of pleasures? He said: death. And he said: are not the believers those who most remember death and most prepare themselves for it?

The Commander of the Faithful, Ali, delivered a sermon and said: I have never seen a certainty of faith more resembling doubt than the state of this man. Every day he bids farewell to the dead and walks in their funeral procession to the graves, and yet he returns to the delusion of the world and does not cease from its desires and pleasures. Were there for the wretched son of Adam no sin to bring him low and no reckoning to be settled against him, the dead alone would summon him to caution, for death scatters what a man has gathered, disperses his assembly, and orphans his children, so that he ought to guard against the state he is in with the utmost guarding. Yet people have grown heedless of death with the heedlessness of a people upon whom it will never descend, and they have leaned upon the world and its desires with the leaning of a people who look for no reckoning and fear no punishment.

**Mentioning Grief at the Time of Death, and Condolence**

When Ibrahim, the son of the Messenger of Allah (ص), died, the Prophet ordered Ali to wash him, and the Messenger of Allah (ص) shrouded him. Then he said: O Ali, carry him. So he carried him until he brought him to al-Baqi, and the Messenger of Allah (ص) prayed over him. Then he said: O Ali, carry him. So he carried him until he brought him to the grave. Then he said: O Ali, go down into the grave. So he went down into it, and the Messenger of Allah (ص) lowered him to him. When he saw him laid in his place, he wept, and the people wept at his weeping, until the voices of the men rose above the voices of the women. Then the Messenger of Allah (ص) forbade them most sternly from wailing, and said: the eye sheds tears and the heart grieves, but do not say what angers the Lord. Indeed we are stricken by losing you, and indeed we sorrow over you, O Ibrahim.

He (ص) said: I do not forbid you to weep, but I forbid you from lamentation and wailing. This weeping is a mercy that Allah Almighty places in the heart of whom He wills among His creation, and Allah shows mercy to whom He wills, and Allah shows mercy only to the merciful among His servants. And he (ص) said: whoever is stricken by a calamity, let him remember his calamity in me, for it is greater than every calamity. And he said: patience is with the first shock, meaning at the moment of bereavement.
