And he said: whoever loses three of his children and bears their loss for the sake of Allah, they will be a shield for him from the Fire. It was said: and two, O Messenger of Allah? He said: and two.

When the Messenger of Allah (ص) was taken, his household heard one speaking whose words they heard but whose person they did not see. He said: peace be upon you, O people of the House, and the mercy of Allah and His blessings. ﴿كُلُّ نَفْسٍ ذَائِقَةُ الْمَوْتِ وَإِنَّمَا تُوَفَّوْنَ أُجُورَكُمْ يَوْمَ الْقِيَامَةِ فَمَن زُحْزِحَ عَنِ النَّارِ وَأُدْخِلَ الْجَنَّةَ فَقَدْ فَازَ وَمَا الْحَيَاةُ الدُّنْيَا إِلَّا مَتَاعُ الْغُرُورِ﴾ [آل عمران: ١٨٥]. Indeed in Allah is a consolation from every calamity and a replacement for everything that perishes. So in Allah place your hope, and Him alone worship, and know that the one truly afflicted is the one deprived of reward. And peace be upon you, and the mercy of Allah and His blessings.

The Messenger of Allah (ص) used to take from the women, when receiving their oath of allegiance, that they would not wail. And he (ص) said: three practices of the age of ignorance will not cease among the people until the Day of Resurrection: seeking rain by the stars, impugning lineages, and wailing over the dead. Jafar ibn Muhammad enjoined at his death, saying: do not strike your cheeks for me, and do not tear your garments for me. And the Commander of the Faithful, Ali, wrote to his judge over al-Ahwaz: beware of wailing over the dead in any land in which you hold authority.

**Mentioning the Washing of the Deceased**

The Messenger of Allah (ص) charged Ali to undertake his washing, and said to him: Jibrail will be with you to turn me, and let al-Fadl ibn Abbas pass by so that he may lower his gaze and hand you the water, for none may see my nakedness but you. So Ali washed him, and he said: I was being helped with him, and I felt a hand turning him with me which I could not see. When I began to wash him, I wished to remove the shirt from him, but I was called to: do not remove the shirt from him. So he was washed in his shirt. And I wished to lay him face down, but I was called to: do not lay him face down. So I turned him upon his side.

He was washed three washings: a washing with water and cleansing herb, which is the soapwort with which the deceased is likewise washed, or with what stands in its place such as the lote-tree leaf and marshmallow and the like; a washing with water and camphor; and a washing with pure water. Upon this the deceased should be washed, according to the teaching of the Ahl al-Bayt: three washings, a washing with water and lote-tree leaf, a washing with water and camphor, and the third and last with pure water. He is turned upon his side, and he is not laid upon his face, nor is he seated upright as the common people do with the dead when they prop the deceased up and set him sitting.

The washing of the deceased is like the washing from major ritual impurity. One begins by washing his hands, then his private parts, and the washer wraps a cloth around his hand when he washes the private parts. He wipes the belly gently at that point while pouring water over the private parts, so that whatever may be there comes out. He washes the face three times and the hands to the elbows, and wipes the head and the feet, but he does not put water into his mouth or nose. When he wipes his head, he wipes his ears. Then he pours the water over him and passes his hand over the rest of his body, and turns him so that the water reaches what is beneath him. He washes him unclothed, covering what is between his navel and his knees with a wrapper; and if he washes him in a garment, there is no harm in it. He cleanses whatever is upon him of smear and filth, and rubs the soles of his feet if there is dirt upon them with a shard or a stone, and removes any dirt that may be beneath the nails. He washes the head and the beard with lote-tree leaf and takes care in cleaning him and removing every smear and defilement from him; and if there is need to heat the water for his washing, it is heated. He is washed thus three times, as we have described.

The washer should conceal the nakedness of the deceased, be gentle with him, keep secret whatever he sees of him, and not mention anything he observes in him. The Messenger of Allah (ص) said: there is no Muslim man who washes his Muslim brother without finding him foul, without looking upon his nakedness, and without mentioning any evil of him, then walks in his procession, and prays over him, and sits until he is placed in his grave, but that he emerges fragrant, cleansed of his sins.

We have narrated from the Imams of the Ahl al-Bayt of the Messenger of Allah (ص) that they commanded the washing of all Muslims, the people of disobedience and others alike, and that they permitted one in a state of major impurity and the menstruating woman to wash the dead. They said: a man washes men and a woman washes women; a woman may wash her husband and a man may wash his wife, when there is need, from behind a garment. If a woman dies among men, or a man among women, and there is no mahram (مَحْرَم) among them and none is found to wash them, they are buried and not washed. The martyr is buried in the garments in which he was stricken and is not washed, and his weapon, leather, and fur are removed from him. This is when he dies on the field of battle; but if he is carried off and reaches his people and then dies, he is washed and shrouded. The drowned, the burned, and a limb of a person that is found, when it is such that a man could not live once it left him, are washed and buried. Whatever falls from the deceased is washed and placed with him; and if his limbs, or any part of them, become separated from his body, each is washed and each limb set in its place, and he is shrouded thus and buried. It is recommended to hasten the burial of the deceased, except the drowned and the one struck senseless and whoever is doubtful in his case, for one waits with him until the truth of his death is known. If a man dies while in major impurity, or a woman while menstruating or in childbed, the washing suffices for them, just as any other who dies in purity is washed.

**Mentioning the Embalming and the Shrouds**

We have narrated from the Imams of the Ahl al-Bayt that when the washer of the deceased has finished his washing, he should dry him with a cloth, take a length of cloth of the measure by which he may gird him, spread cotton upon him, and set embalming camphor upon it. He removes, with gentleness and modesty, the cloth with which the nakedness was covered, binds this cloth upon the loins, and places the cotton he has spread over the private parts and the seat, binding it well upon him. He clothes him in a shirt, if that is his shroud or one of his garments; and if that is not available, he leaves him so upon the washing-place.

Then the shrouds are spread out and their edges are set in order, and he lifts him and lays him within them. The embalming is placed on the places of prostration: upon his forehead and his nose, in the palms of his hands, and on the fronts of his knees and his feet. Then the folds of the shroud are drawn over him, and he is wound with a turban, the middle of which is set upon his head and returned beneath his chin, and its tail let down upon his chest with his shrouds. Of the embalming is placed upon his head, and in his hearing and his sight, and upon his nose and his beard and his chest. The embalming and whatever is of sweet scent are placed therein; but neither wars nor saffron is placed in the embalming of men, though it is placed in the embalming of women. The Commander of the Faithful, Ali, placed in the embalming of the Messenger of Allah (ص) musk which he had charged him to place in it, and what remained over of it, the Messenger of Allah (ص) commanded Ali to place in his own embalming. Camphor is placed in the embalming.

The shrouds of the deceased and the place of his washing are perfumed with incense, but the censer is not carried behind his bier. One in a state of ritual consecration is not brought near any scent, just as scent is not brought near him while living. It is recommended that the number of the winding-sheets for the deceased be odd, three or five or seven, and there is no fixed limit set for that. A single garment suffices as a shroud, just as it suffices in the prayer. They disliked that a man be shrouded in pure silk, and they permitted shrouding in dyed garments. The shroud is taken from the principal of the estate the deceased has left.

A believer who has died is prepared for his final journey with care and dignity. We have narrated from the Imams of the Ahl al-Bayt that once the deceased has been shrouded he is to be placed upon a bier if one can be found, and carried upon the shoulders of men wherever this is possible. If no bier is available, he may be carried upon an animal or by whatever means can be found, and his shroud may be covered over with a garment. Over a woman's bier a coffin is set in the form of a dome and draped with a garment, and the deceased is tied down if there is any fear that the body might slip from the bier. The procession moves at a brisk and steady pace, neither hurried into disorder nor dragged slowly along as the Jews did. Whoever wishes to carry the bier begins by taking its front right side upon his right shoulder, then turns about it if he wishes, and the others take their turns in bearing it. Adorning the coffin is disliked. Walking ahead of the funeral is disliked, and walking behind it is what is commanded.

When the procession reaches the graveyard, the bier is set down crosswise with the deceased laid upon his back, his right side toward the qibla and his left toward the one who prays. The one who leads the prayer stands over the bier and pronounces five takbirs, raising his hands with each one, and gives the salutation to his right and his left as he does in the ritual prayer. In the first takbir he praises Allah and extols Him as He is worthy. In the second he prays upon the Prophet and his family. In the third he supplicates for the deceased, striving hard in that supplication if he knew him, saying: "O Allah, this is Your servant and the son of Your servant. He has departed from this world and come to You, and we know of him only good, and I have come as an intercessor for him." Then he prays for him with whatever supplication he is able. If he did not know the deceased he says: "O Allah, You know best his secret and his intention. If he was a doer of good, then increase him in his good and have mercy upon him; and if he was a sinner, then forgive him and pardon him." He labors in supplication and says: "Our Lord, You have encompassed all things in mercy and knowledge, so forgive those who repent and follow Your way," to the end of the verse, "and that is the great triumph." If he wishes he may say: "O Allah, You know this servant of ours better than we do, so gather him with those he loved, and give his charge to the one who had authority over him," and the like of this. And if the deceased is a child, he says: "O Allah, make him a forerunner for his parents, a reward and a treasure laid up in advance, and let them and us profit through him." One supplicates in a case such as this with whatever he is able. In the fourth takbir he supplicates for the believing men and women, and in the fifth he prays upon the Prophet (صَلَّى اللَّهُ عَلَيْهِ وَعَلَى آلِهِ) and his family, and then he withdraws. He keeps all of this quiet except the takbir, which he announces aloud; and if he prays over the deceased at night he raises his voice, as one raises it in the prayers of the night.

There is a second narration from Abu Jafar Muhammad ibn Ali (ع). He said: the one praying over the deceased praises Allah and extols Him in the first takbir, prays upon the Prophet and his family in the second, supplicates for the Imam in the third and for the believers in the fourth, supplicates for the deceased in the fifth if he is one who is known, and then prays upon the Prophet and his family and gives the salutation. He said: in like manner the Messenger of Allah (صَلَّى اللَّهُ عَلَيْهِ وَعَلَى آلِهِ) once pronounced only four takbirs over one whom he prayed over from among the hypocrites, who did not deserve to be supplicated for; they departed after the fourth takbir, did not supplicate for him, and left his corpse cast aside.

The prayer over the deceased may be offered at any hour of the day or night except at the moment of the sun's rising and its setting, and it is better that it be prayed by day. Funerals should not be prayed over at night unless one is compelled to it, for it has come from the Messenger of Allah (صَلَّى اللَّهُ عَلَيْهِ وَعَلَى آلِهِ) that he said: "Bury your dead by day, for the angels of the day are more merciful than the angels of the night." If the ruler, or one whom the ruler has appointed over the congregational funeral prayer, is present, he has the better right to come forward and lead the prayer over the deceased than the guardian; and where there is no ruler, it is the nearest of the guardians, then the next nearest. The prayer is offered over the martyr, over those guilty of grave sins and transgressions, over the one put to death by a prescribed penalty or in retaliation, over the child born of adultery, and over a severed limb if it is known that its owner cannot live once it is parted from the body; but if that is not known, no prayer is offered over the limb apart from the rest of the body.

When several funerals are brought together, the leader may pray over them all at once if he wishes, ranging them in a single row if they are all men or all women, and standing in the middle of them; and even where there is only one, he stands at the chest of a man and at the head of a woman. If he prays over them one by one, that too is permitted. When funerals of men and women are gathered, the women are set nearest the qibla and the men beyond them; and likewise when there is a single man and a single woman, the woman is set nearest the qibla and the man beyond her. Whoever joins the leader after the takbir has begun enters the prayer with him, begins with whatever supplication he ought to begin with, and follows the leader's takbirs; and when the leader has finished, the latecomer makes up what he missed, then gives the salutation and withdraws, just as he does when he comes late to the ritual prayer. Whoever does not reach the leader before the salutation has missed the funeral prayer; he may supplicate for the deceased if he wishes, but he does not offer the prayer over him.

As for the burial itself, we have narrated from the Imams of the Ahl al-Bayt that they commanded graves to be dug for the dead and forbade digging them deeper than three cubits. Then a niche, the lahd, is cut into the grave, or a trench, the darih, is dug, whichever is preferred. The lahd is a recess hollowed out at the side toward the qibla to receive the body where it will lie, while the ground behind it, toward the rear of the grave, is left, so that one who prepares the niche may stand upon it as he works. The darih is a hollow cut down the middle of the grave, with ledges before it and behind it. Whichever of these is done is permitted and good, and they allowed the niche to be lined when there is need for it.

When the deceased is brought to his grave and set down at its edge on the side toward the qibla, his guardians descend into the grave, or those whom they appoint to lower him. The one nearest to a man takes his place at the front, and the one nearest to a woman at her rear. They go down into the grave and come up again from the side of the deceased's feet and receive him, taking hold of him with their own hands. If it is a woman, a garment is spread over the grave to screen her while she is lowered, until she is hidden from view. They lay the body upon its right side, set evenly, not tilted onto its face nor onto its back, and they loosen the knots that were tied upon the shroud and supplicate for the deceased with whatever supplication is present with them. They set up the unbaked bricks over him, or planks, or palm branches if they wish; and if they heap the earth over him directly, that is good. They press the earth down over him from above, and those who went down to prepare the niche climb out. The earth taken from the grave is returned into it, and what remains is left mounded upon it, and water is sprinkled over it from above, and then the people withdraw.

If there is need to bury two, or a group, in a single grave, they are buried together, and the most excellent of them is placed nearest the qibla; and if there are men and women, the men are set in front of the women, with a little earth laid between each two to divide them. They regarded moving the dead from one place to another as disliked, and were severe in the matter. They directed that the grave be made square and level and not raised into a peaked mound, and they permitted that it be marked with a sign so that it may be recognized, and that it be plastered, whitened, and roofed over with a covering. They permitted the visiting of the grave from time to time across the length of the years, with mercy sought for the deceased and forgiveness asked for him, and they commanded whoever enters the burial ground to greet the people of the graves and supplicate for them all. They forbade stepping across the graves, laughing beside them, praying between them, and relieving oneself among them.

If a polytheist woman dies and there is in her womb a child fathered by a Muslim which has not yet come forth from her, her own people take charge of her and she is buried in their graveyards. If a woman dies and there is in her womb a living child that stirs, there is no harm in the midwife reaching in with her hand and delivering the child without cutting the mother open, if she is able; and if she cannot, she opens the belly and brings the child out. If no midwife can be found for this, then the man closest of kin to her may do it. But this is done only when the child's life is certain, that it has reached six months or more, at which age it is known to live if it is delivered; otherwise it is left until death overtakes it in the womb, and she is not to be buried while it still stirs within her. Likewise, if the child dies in the woman's womb and she is endangered by it, the midwife reaches in with her hand, severs it, and removes it, for a belly has been opened for other than a child and the mother has emerged alive; and to open the belly of the dead child is more fitting than to kill the living child in its mother's womb.

They held it proper that food be sent to the family of the deceased for as long as they remain in their grief, this being done for them by their neighbors and those closest to them, since grief so occupies them that they are kept from preparing food for themselves. The Messenger of Allah (صَلَّى اللَّهُ عَلَيْهِ وَعَلَى آلِهِ) commanded his household to prepare food for the family of Jafar ibn Abi Talib when the news of his death reached them. Al-Numan said: "There has come upon them that which occupies them and turns them away from seeing to this for themselves."

## 6. The Due of Wealth: Zakat

The chapters of zakat open here, and with them the command to give it, the encouragement toward it, and the severity that attends its withholding. Allah the Almighty said: "Successful indeed is he who purifies himself, and remembers the name of his Lord, and prays" [al-Ala]. And He said: "Blessed be the believers who are humble in their prayers, and who turn away from ill speech, and who are active in giving zakat" [al-Muminun]. And He said: "Establish the prayer and pay the zakat." The Almighty said: "When the sacred months have passed, kill the polytheists wherever you find them, and take them, and confine them, and wait for them at every lookout. But if they repent, establish prayer, and pay zakat, then let them go their way" [al-Tawbah: 5]. So Allah does not accept the Islam of a polytheist who withholds zakat, and He said: "Woe to the polytheists who do not pay zakat" [al-Fussilat: 6-7].

The Messenger of Allah (صَلَّى اللَّهُ عَلَيْهِ وَعَلَى آلِهِ) was asked about this verse, "Woe to the polytheists who do not pay zakat," and he said: Allah does not reproach the polytheists for it. Have you not heard His words, "Woe to those who pray, who are heedless of their prayers, who make a show of themselves and withhold the maun"? For the maun is zakat. Then he said: By the One in Whose hand is the soul of Muhammad, none betrays Allah in anything of the zakat of his wealth except a polytheist.

And he (صَلَّى اللَّهُ عَلَيْهِ وَعَلَى آلِهِ) said: When Allah wills good for a servant, He sends an angel from among the keepers of Paradise, who wipes his breast so that his soul grows generous with zakat. And he said: Whoever gives the zakat of his wealth, let him give it with a willing soul, for he gives only a portion of what Allah has given him, and Allah stores away the rest for him; and whoever gives the zakat of his wealth, its evil has departed from him. And he said: No wealth is ever destroyed on land or sea except through the withholding of zakat from it; so fortify your wealth with zakat, treat your sick with charity, and turn away affliction with supplication. And he said: No one gives the zakat of his wealth and finds his wealth thereby diminished, nor withholds it and finds it thereby increased, nor does any wealth decrease through charity; so give, and do not shrink from it. And he said: Allah the Almighty has tracts of ground that are called the parched wastes, to which is driven whoever withholds Allah's due from his wealth, so that he is left in want among them.

And he (ع) said: Whoever has camels or cattle or sheep and does not pay their zakat will be laid out flat on the Day of Resurrection upon a barren, level plain, and they will trample him with their hooves and gore him with their horns and bite him with their mouths; whenever the last of them has passed over him, the first of them returns to him, until the reckoning of all creation is finished.

From the Commander of the Believers, Ali (ع), it is related that he said: The worshipper has three signs: prayer, zakat, and fasting. And the Prophet (صَلَّى اللَّهُ عَلَيْهِ وَعَلَى آلِهِ) said in his bequest to al-Hasan (ع): I counsel you, O Hasan, and all my children and the people of my house, and whoever among the believers this writing of mine reaches, to fear Allah your Lord. And Allah, Allah, regarding zakat, for it extinguishes the anger of Allah from you.

And he said: Allah the Almighty has made obligatory upon the wealthy, from their wealth, the measure that suffices their poor. If the poor go hungry or naked, it is because of what the wealthy withhold, and Allah the Almighty will hold them to account for that on the Day of Resurrection and punish them severely. And he said: Whoever amasses wealth and does not pay its due, his wealth becomes a serpent that will devour him on the Day of Resurrection. And he said: The withholder of zakat is like the devourer of riba, and no one who withholds zakat is a Muslim.

And he said concerning the words of Allah the Almighty, "They will be encircled by what they were stingy with on the Day of Resurrection" [Al Imran: 180]: The wealth on which zakat was withheld is fashioned into the likeness of a bald serpent that winds about his neck and bites at him until the people are finished with the reckoning, and it says: I am your wealth, whose zakat you withheld. And he said concerning the words of Allah the Almighty, "And those who hoard gold and silver and do not spend it in the way of Allah, give them tidings of a painful punishment, on the day when it will be heated in the fire of Hell, and with it will be branded their foreheads, their sides, and their backs. This is what you hoarded for yourselves, so taste what you used to hoard" [al-Tawbah: 34-35]: The hoard is the wealth on which zakat is not paid; but wealth whose zakat has been paid is no hoard. As the Prophet (صَلَّى اللَّهُ عَلَيْهِ وَعَلَى آلِهِ) said: On the Day of Resurrection there will be brought the wealth of one who withheld its zakat, and it will be beaten into plates, then heated in the fire of Hell, and with them his forehead, his sides, and his back will be branded; whenever they cool, they are heated again and the branding resumed, until the reckoning of all creation is finished. And he said: Whoever pays the zakat of his wealth has discharged what was owed upon him in it.

A man asked Abu Abd Allah Jafar ibn Muhammad ibn Ali (ع) about a man who complained to him that he was seized with terror in his sleep, as though a woman were pressing a demand upon him and frightening him. He said: This is a man who does not pay the zakat of his wealth. The questioner reported this to the man, who said: By Allah, I do pay it. The questioner conveyed that back to Abu Abd Allah (ع), and he said: If that is so, then he does not place it in its proper place. The man said: As for that, yes.

**The payment of zakat to its rightful recipients, and the prohibition of placing it elsewhere.**

> خُذْ مِنْ أَمْوَلِهِمْ صَدَقَةًۭ تُطَهِّرُهُمْ وَتُزَكِّيهِم بِهَا

Allah the Almighty said to Muhammad, His Prophet (صَلَّى اللَّهُ عَلَيْهِ وَعَلَى آلِهِ): "Take from their wealth a charity that will purify them and cleanse them by it" [al-Tawbah: 103]. Then He informed him of the places in which it is to be set, and said: "Charity is only for the poor and the destitute, and those who administer it, and those whose hearts are to be reconciled, and for freeing captives, and for those in debt, and in the cause of Allah, and for the wayfarer, an obligation from Allah, and Allah is All-Knowing, All-Wise" [al-Tawbah: 60].

Jafar ibn Muhammad said: The poor is the needy man who does not beg from people; the destitute is one in even greater hardship, the one who begs from people. Those who administer it are the collectors of the charity who take charge of receiving it from the people. Those whose hearts are to be reconciled are men of enmity who nonetheless hold power and standing and obedience among their people; such a one is given from the zakat so that he may be won over and may incline toward obedience those who follow him, as the Messenger of Allah (صَلَّى اللَّهُ عَلَيْهِ) used to give to the chiefs of the tribes in order to reconcile them, men such as Uyayna ibn Husn and al-Aqra ibn Habis. Their like exists in every age, and whenever they exist the imams give to them from the zakat. "In the freeing of captives" is manumission; "those in debt" are the people burdened with debts; "in the cause of Allah" is in the struggle; and "the wayfarer" is the stranger cut off from his means.

These, then, are eight categories upon whom Allah the Almighty has made the charities obligatory. It is not proper that one category be given the whole of it to the exclusion of another, for there is no dispute among the Muslims on this. Consider: if a man were to name several men by name and set a sum for them, and one of them rose up to claim the whole of it for himself without handing his companions their share, this would not be lawful for him. How much less lawful, then, for one who has heard the words of Allah the Almighty, "Charity is only for the poor and the destitute, and those who administer it," until He has named all eight categories, and who then gives it to a single category among them, when he is bound with this to hand it over to the one whom Allah has commanded to receive it. Allah has made it an obligation upon that one to set it in its proper places, has entrusted to him the oversight of it and the knowledge of the state of each class of its people, and of the measure that should be given at every time and season according to what befalls the Muslims and Islam. This is what none can know or fulfill except the Imams, whom Allah the Almighty has charged with the affairs of His servants and set over His earth and His lands.

The people do not differ that the Messenger of Allah (صَلَّى اللَّهُ عَلَيْهِ وَعَلَى آلِهِ) himself took charge of receiving the charities from those present with him, and sent his agents to receive them from those distant, who then brought them to him so that he might distribute them where Allah commanded them distributed. Allah made this clear and plain in mentioning those who administer the charity in His Book and assigning them a share of it for their labor upon it, just as He said to His Messenger (صَلَّى اللَّهُ عَلَيْهِ وَعَلَى آلِهِ), "Take from their wealth a charity" [al-Tawbah: 103]. When He then took him to Himself, the people did not differ that this obligation remained, undiminished by the passing of the Messenger of Allah (صَلَّى اللَّهُ عَلَيْهِ وَعَلَى آلِهِ); rather they agreed that whoever stands in his place takes charge of receiving it as he received it (صَلَّى اللَّهُ عَلَيْهِ وَعَلَى آلِهِ) and directs it to the mentioned channels. They agreed with Abu Bakr upon fighting those who withheld the zakat, named them the people of apostasy, and held their blood lawful. Abu Bakr, Umar, Uthman, and the Commander of the Believers Ali each took charge of receiving it in their days, none differing with them or disputing it.

When the affair passed to the Banu Umayya and the people saw that they had cut it off for themselves, some held back from paying the zakat to them and turned in alarm to those who remained of the Companions of the Messenger of Allah (صَلَّى اللَّهُ عَلَيْهِ وَعَلَى آلِهِ) and questioned them about it. Every one of them commanded that it be paid to the rulers, and said: upon them is what they have taken on, and upon you is what you have taken on; you are to give it to them, and it is upon them to set it in its proper places. Thus they related from Aisha, Abu Hurayra, Sa'd ibn Abi Waqqas, Abu Said al-Khudri, and Abd Allah ibn Umar, and they related no contrary word from any of the Companions, nor do they relate any disagreement among them. When Ibn Umar was pressed with much questioning, he said: Woe to you. Tell me: if I were to seize thieves and cut off the hands of some of them and leave the rest, would you be in the right? They said no. He said: And if you handed them to the ruler and he did that, would it be upon you? They said no. He said: So too do these matters run. Pay it to them, even if with it they devour the flesh of serpents.

This is the word of the generality and what they relate from their predecessors, though today the whole of them has stripped it away and departed from it altogether, without a proof they have advanced or a cause they have brought. Rather they have entered into the company of those whom they themselves reckoned people of apostasy for withholding from their rulers the zakat of their wealth, just as those others withheld their zakat from Abu Bakr. And for that withholding there is a ground which this is not the place to mention, whose exposition would grow long were it set down.

As for the Ahl al-Bayt (عَلَيْهِمُ السَّلَامُ), they do not hold that zakat is to be paid to any but the Imams of guidance from the household of the Messenger of Allah (صَلَّى اللَّهُ عَلَيْهِ وَعَلَى آلِهِ), whom Allah the Almighty has commanded His servants to obey in His Book and whom He has entrusted with the affairs of His servants. Whoever pays the zakat of his wealth willingly to other than them, it does not suffice for him, and he must pay it to the Imam of his age from among them. But if the imams of injustice overpower him and take it from him by force, that suffices for him. The Commander of the Believers Ali said to one of his agents over al-Jazira: Whoever pays the zakat of his wealth to Muawiya and his agents, of those whom our cavalry and our men have kept at bay, do not credit that to him, but take it from him, for it did not reach him and did not protect him. But where they overpowered a man and took it from him by force, accept his word and do not press him.

And he said: The Messenger of Allah (صَلَّى اللَّهُ عَلَيْهِ وَعَلَى آلِهِ) forbade a man to conceal his zakat from his imam, and said that this is hypocrisy. Allah the Almighty has made clear to His servants, upon the tongue of His Messenger Muhammad (صَلَّى اللَّهُ عَلَيْهِ وَعَلَى آلِهِ), to whom they are to hand their charities, in that He appointed them trustees over it, since He forbade it to them and to none besides. The community is agreed that it is not lawful for the Messenger of Allah (صَلَّى اللَّهُ عَلَيْهِ وَعَلَى آلِهِ) nor for the people of his house. They related that al-Hasan ibn Ali, while a small boy, took a date from the dates of the charity and put it into his mouth, and the Messenger of Allah (صَلَّى اللَّهُ عَلَيْهِ وَعَلَى آلِهِ) looked at him and hastened to him and drew it out of his mouth with its moisture upon it and threw it back among the dates of the charity, and said: We, the people of the house, charity is not lawful for us. So He informed them that they are the trusted ones over it, charged with receiving it and setting it in its proper places, and that this belongs to none besides them, to no one else at all among the people.

In like manner He assigned to them the fifth (اَلْخُمْس) which He assigned to the Messenger of Allah (صَلَّى اللَّهُ عَلَيْهِ وَعَلَى آلِهِ), as a token of their trustworthiness and their standing in the place of the Messenger of Allah (صَلَّى اللَّهُ عَلَيْهِ وَعَلَى آلِهِ) after him in that for his community. This belongs to the Imams (عَلَيْهِمُ السَّلَامُ) among them, and not to the rest, for the rest of them are bound in what is due from them, like the rest of the community, to pay their charities to the Imam, just as the rest of the people are bound, as was the state of the household of the Messenger of Allah (صَلَّى اللَّهُ عَلَيْهِ وَعَلَى آلِهِ) with him in his lifetime. It is for one after another, an honor passed on and a virtue kept unbroken. Whoever has denied their right and been ignorant of their trustworthiness, the least that binds him by the word of the Imams and his own predecessors, when Allah makes their affair manifest and gives them dominion over the one who denied them, is that he pay them the zakat of his wealth in every one of his states. We have already set down what suffices of the proof in this, though we have made it brief, for whoever is granted its understanding, and Allah guides to his right course.

**The zakat of gold, silver, and precious substances.**

We have narrated from the Imams of the Ahl al-Bayt that zakat is not due on gold until it reaches twenty mithqals, with a full year having turned over it in the possession of the one who came by it; nor on silver until it reaches two hundred dirhams, with a full year having turned over it in the possession of the one who came by it. When the year has turned over it in the possession of its owner, then upon the twenty mithqals is half a mithqal, and upon the two hundred dirhams is five dirhams; and this has become a nisab in his hand. Whatever exceeds it bears a quarter of a tenth, if he came by it before the head of the year, due at the head of the year, and he pays it out likewise. If at the head of the year it falls short of this measure, there is no zakat upon it; and whatever exceeds bears zakat by that reckoning. The one is not joined to the other, and no zakat is due on gold or on silver until each of the two reaches the measure we have mentioned.

If a man has one hundred dirhams and ten dinars whose value is one hundred dirhams or more, there is no zakat upon him in that, until the dinars reach twenty dinars and the dirhams reach two hundred dirhams and the year has turned over it, unless he spends some of it before the year in flight from the zakat. If he does that, zakat is upon him. This is like a man who had two hundred dirhams and, when he saw that the zakat was becoming due upon him, spent some of it away.

Whoever converts some of his silver into dinars in order to escape the zakat owed on it, or spends some of his dinars for the same purpose, has done what is not permitted. As for mineral wealth such as pearls and amber, when they are drawn out from their source and their place, the fifth is due upon the one who extracts them, and no zakat falls upon what he keeps back. Once he sells such a thing and the buyer takes possession of it, it becomes like the rest of the buyer's wealth: he assesses its value and pays zakat upon it at the completion of the year, if it is held for trade. There is no zakat upon jewelry or upon mineral goods that are taken for personal use, unless a person takes them as a stratagem to flee from zakat. If that is his intent, the intent invalidates the stratagem and zakat is due. The same rule governs what is drawn from the mines, whether gold, silver, brass, iron, lead, mineral stone, or the like: the fifth is due upon it, and once it passes into circulation it is treated like the rest of one's wealth. Whoever holds debased coinage pays its zakat from it in proportion to the pure metal it contains, giving debased for debased; and if it is copper, iron, lead, or some other base metal with a coating over it, its value is referred back to what it truly is in its origin, joined to the rest of his holdings, and zakat is paid upon the whole.

There is no zakat upon clothing, riding animals, household vessels, or furnishings, so long as they are not held for trade and were not acquired as a means of fleeing from zakat. If they are held for trade, or if they were bought with wealth on which zakat was due and then set aside precisely so that no zakat would be paid, then zakat is due upon them. Whoever buys trade goods assesses their value at the head of the year and pays their zakat, provided the goods are present with him or in a place from which he is not barred. So too with a debt owed to him, if he is not prevented from it: when the year turns upon it, its zakat is due, and it is treated as though it were already in his hands. But if he can reach it only through litigation, and the one who owes it refuses him, he does not pay its zakat until he receives it and it comes into his hand. There is no zakat upon the wealth of a child until he reaches maturity, takes possession of it, and a full year passes upon it in his keeping. Yet if that wealth passes into the hand of an adult who trades with it, zakat is due upon it, and the zakat falls upon the one who trades with it; any loss belongs to him, while the profit belongs to the child. The wealth of a woman's dowry follows the rule of a debt, as we have already explained. Whoever pays out the zakat of his wealth to deliver it, and it is lost before it reaches its due place, must make it good again, for he remains its guarantor until he sets it where it belongs. And whoever dies still owing zakat he did not discharge, it is taken from the principal of his estate, whether or not he left a bequest to that effect, once it is known and established.

As for the zakat of camels, cattle, and sheep, we have narrated from the Imams of the household of the Messenger of Allah ﷺ that there is no zakat upon working camels, those upon which loads are carried or by which irrigation is drawn, whatever their number and however great it grows. But if they are grazing camels, kept at pasture for their increase and their milk, then no zakat is due upon fewer than five. When they reach five and a year has passed upon them, one sheep is due; there is nothing more upon what exceeds five until they reach ten, when two sheep are due; at fifteen, three sheep; at twenty, four sheep. At twenty-five a bint makhad is due, which is a she-camel that has completed a year since her birth and entered the second, her mother having conceived again after her and being now in labor, not yet delivered. If no bint makhad is found, then an ibn labun suffices, which is a male that has completed two years and entered the third, whose mother conceived after him and gave birth, so that she is now giving milk; and this holds up to thirty-five. When one more is added, a bint labun is due, up to forty-five. If one more is added, a hiqqah is due, which is a she-camel that has completed three years, entered the fourth, and become fit to bear and to be covered; and this holds up to sixty. If one more is added, a jadhaa is due, which is a she-camel that has completed four years and entered the fifth; and this holds up to seventy-five. If one more is added, two daughters of labun are due, up to ninety. If one more is added, two hiqqas are due, up to a hundred and twenty. Beyond that, for every forty a bint labun is due, and for every fifty a hiqqah. If the assessor does not find among the camels the exact age that is owed, he takes the age below it together with two sheep or twenty dirhams, or the age above it and returns two sheep or twenty dirhams.

There is no zakat upon working cattle, those by which one plows and draws water, nor upon the grazing cattle kept for increase and milk until they reach thirty. When they reach thirty and a year has passed upon them in the ownership of their owner, a tabi or tabia is due, which is the yearling whose horns have grown even. There is nothing upon what exceeds thirty until forty, when a musinnah is due. From forty to sixty it is one musinnah; at sixty, two tabi's; at seventy, one musinnah and one tabi; at eighty, two musinnahs; at ninety, three tabi's; at a hundred, one musinnah and two tabi's; and so onward, for every thirty a tabi and for every forty a musinnah, with nothing due for what lies between.

There is no zakat upon the domestic sheep, which are those raised within the houses and not sent to pasture, but upon the grazing flock zakat is due once it reaches forty and a year has passed upon it in its owner's keeping. When it reaches forty, one sheep is taken from its owner, and there is nothing upon what exceeds forty until it reaches a hundred and twenty. If it passes that by one or more, two sheep are due, until it reaches two hundred. If it passes two hundred by one or more, three sheep are due; and when the flock grows large, for every hundred a sheep is due.

Concerning what the workers of charity are to do in the districts they oversee and what they are to take, and concerning the wealth of those whose holdings are mingled, we have narrated from the Commander of the Believers, Ali ibn Abi Talib, that he appointed Mikhnaf ibn Sulaym al-Azdi over the zakat of Bakr ibn Wail and wrote him a covenant. In it he said: This is what Ibn Abi Talib charged upon Mikhnaf ibn Sulaym when he sent him to gather the charity. He commanded him to fear Allah his Lord in all his affairs and in the hidden acts of his conduct, where there is no witness over him but Allah and no watchful guardian besides Him. He commanded him not to let his obedience to Allah in what appears outwardly differ from what he keeps concealed, for whoever does that has fallen into treachery, has made light of the right of Allah, and has exposed himself to His displeasure. He commanded him to conduct himself with justice among those over whom he is set, to deal fairly with them in what is apportioned, to meet them with an open face and a gentle side, to secure for the wronged among them redress from the one who wronged him, and to hold the balance even among them in his seat, his countenance, his bearing, and his leave to approach, so that no man among them might see any favoring of another over himself. He commanded him not to revile them, nor to scowl upon them, nor to turn from them out of a sense of superiority through the authority he held over them, for they are brothers in religion and helpers against the enemy and aids in the drawing out of what is owed. And he said: You have in this charity, O Mikhnaf ibn Sulaym, a portion and an appointed right, and you have partners in it: the poor, the needy, and the weak of household. If they render you your right, then render them theirs; and if you do not, you will be among the people most beset by adversaries and misery on the Day of Judgment. Woe to the man whose adversary is the poor, the destitute, the ruined, and the wayfarer.

It is narrated from him that the Messenger of Allah (صَلَّى اللَّهُ عَلَيْهِ وَعَلَى آلِهِ) forbade that people be made to swear over their charity, and said of them that they are trusted, meaning trusted as to what is hidden and concealed of their livestock; but what is manifest is taken from them. He said: the one who transgresses in charity is like the one who withholds it, and he forbade that people be dealt with harshly in the matter of charity. The Commander of the Believers, Ali, said: the charity of the people of the open country is taken in their own places, and they are not driven from place to place; and in the year of drought they are granted delay until the land grows green again. It is narrated from him that the Messenger of Allah (صَلَّى اللَّهُ عَلَيْهِ وَعَلَى آلِهِ) forbade the joining of what is separate and the separating of what is joined for the sake of charity. The separating of what is joined is that a man has a hundred and twenty sheep, upon which only a single sheep is due until the number increases; the assessor is forbidden to break them into groups of forty and take three sheep from them. Likewise, if a man owns forty, he is not to be required to divide them into two twenties so as to give no zakat upon them. As for the joining of what is separate, it is that three men, each owning forty sheep and not partners in them, should not be combined so that the assessor takes a single sheep, when, held apart, three sheep would be due. So too the assessor is not to combine the flocks of two men upon each of whom, separately, no zakat is due, so that by joining them zakat becomes due. Nor are partners in livestock charged zakat until each of them holds what makes zakat due upon him; when that is so and their shares are equal, it is taken from them accordingly, and if one holds more than another, they settle the difference between themselves by mutual adjustment. This is like two men who share a flock upon which nothing is due until it reaches eighty; when it reaches eighty, two sheep are due, one upon each of them, if the flock is divided between them in halves. But if one owns a third and the other two-thirds, only a single sheep is due, falling upon the owner of the two-thirds, until the flock reaches a hundred and twenty. If one more is added, two sheep are due, and the two partners settle the difference between them, for the assessor took from the whole flock two sheep, while one owned forty and the other eighty. Thus he took from the owner of two-thirds one sheep and a third, though only one sheep was truly owed by him, and from the owner of forty a third of a sheep, though a full sheep was owed by him; so it comes to rest at one sheep upon each, the shortfall in each one's share being made good against what the assessor took from him.

Upon this same footing runs the rule of all wealth, whether coin, camels, cattle, sheep, grain, or anything upon which zakat is due: when it is held among partners, each partner owes upon his share only what he would have owed were he no partner at all, because partnership does not dissolve ownership nor transfer any part of it from one to another. Each partner has only what he owns; whatever was due upon him before the partnership is what remains due upon him after it. And when men of wealth gather their properties and mingle them without a true partnership between them, from each is taken what is due upon him, and no regard is given to the mingling of their holdings.

Whoever holds a quorum of wealth on which zakat has become due, and a year passes upon it, and he pays what is owed, and then some addition comes to it, joins that addition to it at the head of the second year and pays zakat upon the whole together. Of coin he gives out a quarter of a tenth; of livestock, the increase is reckoned within the count of the herd. If a mother gives birth while the assessor is present over the flock, he does not reckon the newborn, nor take it, nor take its like in charity. He takes from camels and cattle only the ages that have been described, and from sheep only the full-grown; he does not take the aged, nor the defective, nor the male of the flock, nor its choicest, nor its poorest, but takes from the middling. If the assessor and the owners of the wealth fall into dispute, the owners choose a third of the flock and set it apart, and the assessor chooses from the remaining two-thirds what is due and takes it.

As for the zakat of grains and fruits, Allah, mighty and majestic, has said: ﴿وَهُوَ الَّذِى أَنشَأَ جَنَّاتٍ مَّعْرُوشَاتٍ وَغَيْرَ مَعْرُوشَاتٍ وَالنَّخْلَ وَالزَّرْعَ مُخْتَلِفًا أُكُلُهُ وَالزَّيْتُونَ وَالرُّمَّانَ مُتَشَابِهًا وَغَيْرَ مُتَشَابِهٍ كُلُوا مِن ثَمَرِهِ إِذَا أَثْمَرَ وَآتُوا حَقَّهُ يَوْمَ حَصَادِهِ﴾ [Al-Anam: 141]. It is narrated from Jafar ibn Muhammad concerning the words of Allah, mighty and majestic, ﴿وَآتُوا حَقَّهُ يَوْمَ حَصَادِهِ﴾ [Al-Anam: 141], that he said: this is over and above the obligatory charity; it is given at the time of harvest to the one who asks, who is given the handful, that is, the fistful, and the thing given in charity alongside the imposed obligation. It is narrated from him, from his forefathers, that the Messenger of Allah (صَلَّى اللَّهُ عَلَيْهِ وَعَلَى آلِهِ) made it clear beyond all clarity that the tenth is due upon all that the sky and the rivers water, and half the tenth upon what is watered by wells. It has come from the household that the tenth is due upon all grains once they have set and ripened; at that point they are appraised, when they first appear, and their owners are given a just estimate. They are then left free to their crop and are held to a tenth of what was estimated. The one who makes the estimate is charged not to press it to the utmost, but to leave something aside. They said: the Messenger of Allah (صَلَّى اللَّهُ عَلَيْهِ وَعَلَى آلِهِ) sent Abdullah ibn Rawaha al-Ansari to the people of Khaybar, having been his agent among them upon the half, when the fruit began to ripen. He estimated it upon them, and they objected. So Abdullah ibn Rawaha al-Ansari said to them: I have made my estimate and have not laid more upon you than is right. If you wish, render to us half of what we have estimated; and if you wish, hand it over to us and take half of it. The Jews said: This is the truth by which the heavens and the earth stand. As for honey, they said: if it is held for trade, zakat is due upon it; but what is bought of it, and what is drawn from the combs and cut from the hives, carries no zakat.

As for the zakat of the breaking of the fast, Allah, mighty and majestic, has said: ﴿قَدْ أَفْلَحَ مَن تَزَكَّى وَذَكَرَ اسْمَ رَبِّهِ فَصَلَّى﴾ [Al-Ala]. Jafar ibn Muhammad said of His words, "He has prospered who purifies himself," that it means he who gives the zakat of the breaking of the fast; and of "and mentions the name of his Lord and prays," that it means the prayer of the festival. He said that the zakat of the breaking of the fast is a duty upon the rich and the poor alike. The Messenger of Allah (صَلَّى اللَّهُ عَلَيْهِ وَعَلَى آلِهِ) said that the charity of the breaking of the fast is due upon every soul, for himself and for all his dependents, young and old, free and enslaved, a sa of food from each one of them. It is narrated from Jafar ibn Muhammad that he said: a man pays the zakat of the breaking of the fast on behalf of his Jewish and Christian slave. It is narrated from him that he said: if the slaves of a man's wife are among his dependents, he pays on their behalf; and if they are not among his dependents, then the wife must pay on their behalf. And he used to pay the zakat of the breaking of the fast on behalf of his father after his father's death, and this is a voluntary good, not an obligation. He said that one who cannot find food gives in its place dirhams to the value current at the time, and that it is the way of the sunnah to pay the zakat of the breaking of the fast before the festival prayer.

The zakat upon honey follows the same principle that governs merchandise. If the honey is held for trade, then zakat is due upon it. But what a man buys for himself, or draws from his own hives, or cuts from the combs, carries no zakat.

There remains the zakat of the breaking of the fast, the zakat al-fitr. Allah, Mighty and Majestic, has said: ﴿قَدْ أَفْلَحَ مَن تَزَكَّىٰ وَذَكَرَ اسْمَ رَبِّهِ فَصَلَّىٰ﴾. Jafar ibn Muhammad explained this verse. His words "He has indeed succeeded who purifies himself" mean the one who pays the zakat al-fitr, and "and remembers the name of his Lord and prays" means the prayer of the Eid.

He said that the zakat al-fitr is obligatory upon the rich and the poor alike. And the Messenger of Allah (صَلَّى اللَّهُ عَلَيْهِ وَعَلَى آلِهِ) said that the sadaqat al-fitr is due upon every person, on behalf of himself and on behalf of all his dependents, the young among them and the old, the free and the slave. For each one of them the measure is a sa of food.

From Jafar ibn Muhammad it is related that he said a man pays the zakat al-fitr on behalf of his slave, whether that slave be a Jew or a Christian. And from him it is related that he said, when a man's wife has slaves who are counted among his dependents, he pays on their behalf; but if they are not among his dependents, then it falls upon the wife to pay on their behalf.

He used to pay the zakat al-fitr on behalf of his father after his father's death. This is a voluntary act of goodness, not an obligation.

He said that whoever does not find food should give in its place dirhams according to the value at the time. And the sunnah is that the zakat al-fitr be paid before the fast is broken, before the Eid.

## 7. The Fast: Ramadan and the Discipline of Abstention

Fasting in the month of Ramadan is a duty of its own, and it is fitting to set out what the fast is, what is desired in it, how it begins, what breaks it, and how it stands during travel and illness.

**On fasting and what is desired in it**

Allah Almighty said: "O you who believe, fasting has been prescribed for you as it was prescribed for those before you, so that you may become God-fearing, for an appointed number of days," continuing to His words, "so that you may complete the number and glorify Allah for having guided you, that perhaps you will be grateful." [al-Baqarah]

By this Allah Almighty made fasting the month of Ramadan an obligation, every year, upon every person of legal responsibility. I have mentioned in the chapters on prayer what boys are commanded regarding prayer and fasting, and in what circumstances they are commanded to it.

It is narrated from Jafar ibn Muhammad (ع) that when the month of Ramadan drew near he would supplicate, saying: "O Allah, the blessed month of Ramadan has come. If You bring us to it, help us through it and accept it from us." And when it entered he would say in his supplication: "O Allah, this is the month of Ramadan in which the Quran was sent down as guidance for the people, and as clear signs of guidance and the Criterion. O Allah, bless us in the month of Ramadan, help us through it, and accept it from us, for You are capable of all things."

It is narrated from his father (ع) that he said: "Do not say Ramadan alone, but say as Allah Almighty said, the month of Ramadan." And from his forefathers, that the Messenger of Allah (ص) said: "Shaban is my month, and Ramadan is the month of Allah, and it is the springtime of the poor." By this he pointed to its excellence. All months belong to Allah Almighty, yet the month of Ramadan is attributed to Him in honor, and his saying "Shaban is my month" is likewise from the perspective of honoring it.

The Prophet (ص) used to fast. He ascended the pulpit and said: "O people, Jibril came to me and said, O Muhammad, whoever reaches the month of Ramadan and is not forgiven in it, then dies, may Allah keep him far away. Say Amin." So I said, "Amin." And he (ص) preached to the people on the last day of Shaban and said: "O people, a great and blessed month has cast its shade over you, a month in which one night is better than a thousand months. Whoever draws near to Allah in it through any quality of goodness is as one who performs an obligatory duty in another month, and whoever performs an obligatory duty in it is as one who performs seventy obligatory duties in another month. It is the month of patience, and the reward of patience is Paradise. It is the month of consolation, a month in which the sustenance of the believer is increased. Whoever provides a fasting person with what breaks his fast earns forgiveness of his sins and the freeing of his neck from the Fire, and he receives the like of that fasting person's reward, without anything being diminished from the fasting person's reward."

Some of them said, "O Messenger of Allah, not all of us find what to give a fasting person to break his fast with." He (ص) said that Allah Almighty grants this reward even to one who breaks a fasting person's fast with a draft of milk, or a date, or a drink of water. And whoever satisfies a fasting person, Allah will give him to drink from the Basin a drink after which he will never thirst until he enters Paradise. Its first part is mercy, its middle is forgiveness, and its last is freedom from the Fire. Whoever lightens the burden of the one his right hand possesses in it, Allah forgives him and frees him from the Fire. He said further: "So increase in it four qualities, two by which you will please your Lord, and two of which you can have no need to be without. As for the two by which you please your Lord, they are the testimony that there is no god but Allah alone, with no partner, and that you seek His forgiveness. As for the two of which you cannot do without, they are that you ask Allah for Paradise and seek refuge in Him from the Fire."

> إِنَّمَا ٱلصَّدَقَتُ لِلْفُقَرَآءِ وَٱلْمَسَكِينِ وَٱلْعَمِلِينَ عَلَيْهَا وَٱلْمُؤَلَّفَةِ قُلُوبُهُمْ وَفِى ٱلرِّقَابِ وَٱلْغَرِمِينَ وَفِى سَبِيلِ ٱللَّهِ وَٱبْنِ ٱلسَّبِيلِ ۖ فَرِيضَةًۭ مِّنَ ٱللَّهِ ۗ وَٱللَّهُ عَلِيمٌ حَكِيمٌۭ

He (ص) said: "For everything there is a zakat, and the zakat of bodies is fasting." And regarding the words of Allah Almighty, "And seek help through patience and prayer," he said: "Patience is fasting," and Allah gives the patient their reward without reckoning. He (ص) said to Ali (ع): "O Ali, fast in the heat, complete the ablution fully in the cold, that is, in winter, and teach yourself patience." He also said that the people who will have the most rest at the Standing are those who ate the least in this world, and the hungriest on the Day of Resurrection are those who were the most filled in this world. He (ص) said that Allah Almighty, and all His angels, send blessings upon those who fast, and he said: "I have not commanded My angels to supplicate for anyone without their supplication being answered."

He (ص) said: "The sleep of the fasting person is worship, and his breath is glorification." And he said that Allah Almighty says: "Fasting is for Me, and I Myself reward for it. The fasting person has two joys, a joy when he breaks his fast, and a joy when he meets his Lord. By Him in whose hand is the soul of Muhammad, the odor of the mouth of the fasting believer is more fragrant to Allah than the scent of musk." And when he (ص) ate someone's food, he would say: "May the fasting break their fast with you, may the righteous eat your food, and may the angels send blessings upon you." This points to the excellence of providing for the breaking of the fast.

**On the beginning of the fast and entering it**

> أُحِلَّ لَكُمْ لَيْلَةَ ٱلصِّيَامِ ٱلرَّفَثُ إِلَىٰ نِسَآئِكُمْ

The Messenger of Allah (ص) said: "The pre-dawn meal is a blessing, so take it, even if one of you drinks only a mouthful of water, for Allah has angels who send blessings upon those who take the pre-dawn meal." The Commander of the Believers, Ali, said: When Allah Almighty revealed, "Eat and drink until the white thread becomes distinct to you from the black thread," people took two threads, a white one and a black one, and kept eating and drinking through the night until the white thread became distinct from the black thread. So Allah Almighty revealed, "of dawn." He said: the dawn by which food is forbidden, and which is forbidden to the fasting person, is the dawn whose light spreads across the horizons of the east. The first dawn forbids nothing, and it is the one that rises slender and ascending on high.

He said: the sunnah is to hasten the breaking of the fast, meaning after the setting of the sun, and to delay the pre-dawn meal. He said that the Messenger of Allah (ص) forbade that the obligatory fast be observed except with a firm intention to fast. The Commander of the Believers (ع) said: Allah Almighty made the crescent moons markers for fasting, appointed times for the Hajj, and fixed terms for debts. That is among the blessings of Allah Almighty upon His creation. Whoever fasts upon something other than the sighting of the crescent, and then learns that that day was indeed of the month of Ramadan, must make it up, for he fasted only voluntarily. There is no harm in fasting voluntarily on a day about which there is doubt whether it belongs to the month of Ramadan, intending it as of Shaban. If he later learns it was of Ramadan, he makes it up, because he fasted it voluntarily. One does not fast in obligation upon a day of doubt, for Allah Almighty is not to be worshipped in any of His worship upon a basis of doubt.

**On what breaks the fast and what is required of one who breaks it**

> أُحِلَّ لَكُمْ لَيْلَةَ ٱلصِّيَامِ ٱلرَّفَثُ إِلَىٰ نِسَآئِكُمْ

Allah Almighty said: "It has been made lawful for you, on the night of the fast, to go in unto your wives," continuing to His words, "then complete the fast until the night." [al-Baqarah] The fasting person is thus forbidden food, drink, and intercourse during the daytime of the fast, and this is a matter of consensus among the Muslims. It has come from the Messenger of Allah (ص) that he made expiation obligatory upon whoever deliberately breaks the fast or has intercourse during the daytime of the month of Ramadan. The expiation for one who deliberately breaks the fast or has intercourse during the daytime of Ramadan is the freeing of a slave. If he does not find one, then fasting two consecutive months. If he is unable, then feeding sixty poor persons, together with making up the day on which he broke his fast. If he finds neither a slave to free nor anything to give in charity, and is unable to fast, then nothing is required of him, for Allah does not burden a soul beyond its capacity except with what He has given it.

We have narrated from the Ahl al-Bayt (عَلَيْهِمُ السَّلَامُ) concerning a man who dallies with his wife during the daytime of the month of Ramadan, or kisses her, or touches her, and emits: that he owes both the making-up and the expiation. If he does not emit, there is nothing upon him, though one who fears this for himself ought not to expose himself to it, but should keep his fast pure, guard it from what would corrupt it, and not expose it to what would break it. Whoever goes in unto his wife during the daytime of the month of Ramadan while she is asleep, or coerces her, or she is insane, or she is a child, owes the making-up and the expiation, if he is a responsible adult. There is nothing upon the woman when she is in one of the states we have mentioned.

They said: whoever has intercourse in the night of the month of Ramadan should not deliberately sleep until he has performed the ritual bath. If he does so and rises in a state of major impurity, he owes the making-up of that day. Likewise if he has a nocturnal emission, wakes, and then sleeps again. But if his eyes overcome him, or he became impure and did not wake until dawn broke, he performs the ritual bath, completes his fast, and nothing is upon him. Whoever ate, drank, or had intercourse forgetfully during the daytime of the month of Ramadan should complete his fast, and there is no making-up upon him and no expiation, but he should leave that off the moment he remembers, spitting out whatever food or drink is in his mouth and rinsing it. If, after remembering, he deliberately swallows any of that, or persists in the intercourse, he owes the making-up and the expiation.

They said concerning one who deliberately vomits during the daytime of the month of Ramadan that he makes up a day in its place, on account of his laxity toward his fast. If anything of that vomit returns to his throat, he owes the making-up and the expiation. But if the vomit overcame him and he could not restrain it, nothing is upon him. Whoever rose in the last part of the night and ate or drank while inside his house, without going out and without looking to see whether dawn had risen, and dawn had in fact risen when he ate or drank, must complete his fast and make up a day. But if he had gone out, looked for the dawn, and did not see it risen, then returned and ate or drank while dawn had risen, without intending it, without laxity, and without neglecting to look, then nothing is upon him. Whoever is in doubt about the rising of the dawn and is among those who have knowledge of it may eat and drink until he is certain of its rising. But whoever is in doubt and has no knowledge of the dawn must refrain until the matter becomes certain to him, or until one who has knowledge of it and observes it confirms it. Whoever looks at the sun and judges that it has set, and so eats or drinks in the month of Ramadan, and then the sun appears to him again, should leave off food and drink until it sets, and nothing is upon him.

When the fasting person applies kohl and its taste reaches his throat, he owes the making-up of that day, if he did not know that its taste reaches his throat. But if he knows that its taste reaches his throat and does so deliberately, he owes the making-up and the expiation. And if he applies a kohl whose taste does not reach his throat, nothing is upon him. They permitted the tooth-stick for the fasting person, unless he uses it with a moist twig. For if he does that and its taste reaches his throat, he has broken his fast. So whoever does this deliberately, or exceeds that, owes the making-up and the expiation, and whoever did not intend it owes only the making-up. If he uses a dry twig, nothing is upon him. They also permitted the fasting person to chew gum and food, and to taste with his tongue, so long as nothing of it reaches his throat, just as he is permitted to rinse his mouth with water. As for cupping, if vomiting occurs to him from it, he makes up a day, and if he knows that cupping will bring this upon him, he ought not to expose himself to it unless he is compelled, in which case it is permitted. They also permitted the fasting person to smell perfume, oils, and pleasant scents, but he should not draw any of it in deeply, lest it reach his throat. Likewise he should not pour into his ear anything that would reach his throat.

**On fasting during travel**

Allah Almighty said: "O you who believe, fasting has been prescribed for you as it was prescribed for those before you, that you may become God-fearing, for an appointed number of days, and whoever among you is sick or on a journey, then a number of other days." [al-Baqarah] Allah Almighty thus prescribed the fast of the month of Ramadan upon the resident who is of legal responsibility, and prescribed upon the traveler a number of other days. So it is the traveler's obligation to make up, while resident at home, the fast of whatever he traveled through of the month of Ramadan.

Jafar ibn Muhammad said: The Messenger of Allah (ص) set out on the campaign of Mecca in the month of Ramadan, broke his fast, and commanded those with him to break their fast. It reached him that some of his companions had fasted, and he said: "Have you not in the Messenger of Allah a beautiful example?" Then he stopped on the road, at a place where any of them who wished could see him, took a cup of water, and drank it while they watched. When they saw this, they broke their fast. But a group persisted in their fasting, and the Messenger of Allah (ص) named them the disobedient.

Breaking the fast during the daytime of the month of Ramadan while traveling is therefore permitted, by the Book of Allah Almighty and the Sunnah of His Messenger (ص). Whoever refrains from food and drink while traveling is not counted as fasting, and must make up the number of days he refrained, as Allah Almighty has obligated upon him. And whoever does this, leaving food and drink while traveling without holding it to be a fast and without counting it as such, and then makes up those days while resident, nothing is upon him for having refrained from food and drink while traveling. It has come from many of the Imams (عَلَيْهِمُ السَّلَامُ) that they used to do this.

I have mentioned in the chapters on prayer which travel obligates the breaking of the fast and the shortening of the prayer, and wherever the shortening of the prayers is obligatory, the breaking of the fast is obligatory. It is disliked to travel deliberately in the month of Ramadan without need. When a traveler leaves his home during the month of Ramadan before the sun's decline from its zenith, he must make up that day, but if he leaves after the decline, he does not break his fast that day and does not make it up. Likewise, if he arrives home before the decline and has tasted nothing, he completes his fast for that day and does not make it up, and if he arrives after the decline, he must make it up.

**On breaking the fast for illnesses that arise**

Allah Almighty said: "O you who believe, fasting has been prescribed for you as it was prescribed for those before you, that you may become God-fearing, for an appointed number of days, and whoever among you is sick or on a journey, then a number of other days." [al-Baqarah] I have set out in the chapter before this what follows from the apparent sense of these words of Allah Almighty for one who travels in the month of Ramadan, and the like of it is obligatory for one who is sick in it. Abu Abdillah Jafar ibn Muhammad was asked about the limit of illness at which the fasting person may break his fast, and he said that this is entrusted to the sick person, who is trusted concerning it: if he can bear the fast without harm to himself, let him fast.

**The Fast: Ramadan and the Discipline of Abstention**

**The Dispensation Granted to the Ill**

Allah Almighty said: *"Fasting is prescribed for you as it was prescribed for those before you, that you may fear God, for a fixed number of days… and whoever among you is sick or on a journey, then a number of other days."* [Al-Baqarah] As was clarified in the chapter preceding this one, it is not to be supposed that Allah intends by this only those who travel during the month of Ramadan, for the same obligation rests equally upon those who are ill during it.

Abu Abdullah Jafar ibn Muhammad was asked about the degree of sickness that entitles a fasting person to break the fast. He said that this is entrusted to the sick person himself: if fasting harms him, there is no objection to his breaking it; and if it does not harm him, he should fast. Whatever harms a sick person through fasting is grounds for breaking it.

The Commander of the Believers, Ali ibn Abi Talib, ruled concerning the sick person who cannot fast during Ramadan that he must feed one poor person for every day, half a sa of food. He specified that this applies to the sick person who may despair of recovering from his illness, such as one afflicted with leprosy or a settled, chronic ailment; and likewise the elderly man who is unable to fast must feed the poor, since he too may despair of ever being able to make up the missed days. But one who knows that he may recover from his illness and regain the ability to fast must make up the missed days once he is able.

As for the pregnant woman and the woman who is nursing, they are treated in the same manner as the sick: if either is unable to fast, she breaks her fast, even should her condition persist until another Ramadan arrives. If she is then able to fast, she fasts and also feeds the poor for the days she broke before that Ramadan; if she is still unable, she breaks that Ramadan's fast as well, making up the first missed days through feeding the poor and the second through fasting.

All of this is the position of the Ahl al-Bayt. They held it beloved that whoever dies owing obligatory fasting should have it made up on his behalf. They also said that a woman who menstruates during Ramadan and breaks her fast is obligated to make up what she missed. Whoever owes make-up days for Ramadan may observe them as he wishes, consecutively or separately, for Allah has said only, *"then a number of other days."* They disliked making up the days of Ramadan during Dhu al-Hijjah, since that is a month of devotion, and they preferred that the missed days be made up as soon as possible after Ramadan itself. Nothing is incumbent upon one who delays the make-up, so long as it falls before the next Ramadan comes; but if the next Ramadan arrives before the make-up has been performed, then feeding the poor is required in addition, as has been mentioned.

**On Breaking the Fast**

Allah Almighty said: *"Then complete the fast until the night."* [Al-Baqarah: 187] The night begins with the setting of the disc, that is, the disappearance of the sun below the horizon at sunset with no barrier obstructing it. If a barrier does obstruct the view, then the sign of the disc's setting is the darkening of the eastern horizon and the advance of night from that direction, with the redness departing from it.

We have narrated from the Ahl al-Bayt that they loved to hasten the breaking of the fast and disliked delaying it. They instructed that a man break his fast after the Maghrib prayer as promptly as he is able, when he has food at hand, and that he not needlessly remain fasting. They said that if the food is present and the sun has set, he begins with the food before the prayer; but if no food is present, the fasting person prays and then breaks his fast. It is not fitting for him, when the food is set before him, to leave it and rise to pray; rather he eats, then rises and prays while the time still remains.

When the Messenger of Allah (ص) broke his fast, he would say: *"O Allah, for You we have fasted, and upon Your provision we have broken our fast, so accept from us. The thirst has gone, the veins are filled, and the reward remains, if Allah wills."*

They said that if the crescent of Ramadan is sighted during the daytime, whether at the beginning of the day or at its end, the people should not break their fast until night, for that day is counted as a day of Ramadan; this is intended for the sighting at the end of the month. Likewise, if the crescent of the new month is seen during the daytime and had not been sighted the night before, then that day should not be fasted as part of Ramadan, since a day belongs to the month only when its crescent has been sighted on the night preceding it.

**On Laylat al-Qadr**

> إِنَّآ أَنزَلْنَهُ فِى لَيْلَةِ ٱلْقَدْرِ

Allah Almighty said: *"Indeed, We sent it down on the Night of Power,"* to the end of the surah. [Al-Qadr] And He, Blessed and Exalted, said: *"Ha Mim. By the clear Book. Indeed, We sent it down on a blessed night. Indeed, We were warning. In it every wise matter is distinguished, a command from Us. Indeed, We were sending."* [Al-Dukhan]

Abu Jafar Muhammad ibn Ali (ع) said, concerning the words of Allah Almighty, *"The angels and the Spirit descend in it by the permission of their Lord,"* that the angels and the scribes descend to the lowest heaven by the permission of their Lord, and there they write down what is to be in the year of the affairs of men and what shall befall the servants. This matter has, with Allah, a station in which His will operates: He advances what He wills and delays what He wills and confirms what He wills, and with Him is the Mother of the Book. And Jafar ibn Muhammad said, concerning His words *"in it every wise matter is distinguished,"* that on that night the affairs of the year and all that is to occur in it are written.

The Messenger of Allah (ص) was asked about Laylat al-Qadr and said: *"Seek it in the last ten nights of Ramadan."* He also said that on Laylat al-Qadr the roll of pilgrims is written for each year, and in it every wise matter is distinguished. Jafar ibn Muhammad ibn Ali gave a sign of the Night of Power: a wind blows on that night, and if the season is cold it grows warm, and if it is hot it grows cool. The Commander of the Believers related that the Messenger of Allah (ص) would awaken his household during the last ten nights of Ramadan, tighten his waistband, and pass the nights standing in prayer. Jafar ibn Muhammad explained that "tightening his waistband" means that he withdrew from his wives.

**On the Sunnah and Supererogatory Fasting, from Majlis al-Bayan**

Fasting is divided into four kinds: obligatory fasting, Sunnah fasting, forbidden fasting, and optional fasting.

The obligatory kind has seven divisions: the fast of the month of Ramadan every year; the fast of two consecutive months for one who deliberately breaks a fast on one of its days and cannot find a slave to free; the fast of two consecutive months for one who kills a believer by mistake when he cannot find a slave to free while paying the blood money to the family; the fast of two consecutive months in the expiation of zihar for one who cannot find a slave to free; the fast of three days in expiation for a violated oath for one who can find neither food, nor clothing, nor a slave to free; the fast of one who joins Umrah to Hajj in the tamattu pilgrimage when he cannot find a sacrificial offering, which is ten days; and the fast undertaken by vow.

The Sunnah kind has twelve divisions: the fast of itikaf; the fast of Shaban; fasting a Thursday in every month; fasting the three white days; fasting Friday joined with the Thursday or the Saturday, without singling out Friday alone for the fast; the fast in the manner of Dawud (ع), which is one day of fasting followed by one day of breaking the fast, and which is the most rigorous form of fasting; the fast of the day of Arafah for one who is not a pilgrim; the fast of the boy when he reaches nine years of age; the fast that is the expiation for hunting; and the fast that is the expiation for shaving the head.

The forbidden kind has ten divisions: the first is the fast of Eid al-Fitr; the second is the fast of Eid al-Nahr; the third, fourth, and fifth are the fasts of the three days of Tashriq; the sixth is the fast of wisal, which is to join two or more days together without breaking the fast; the seventh is fasting in perpetuity; the eighth is fasting while menstruating; the ninth is fasting by a vow made in disobedience; and the tenth is fasting in doubt, since it is not permitted to fast except upon certainty.

The optional kind has twelve divisions, in each of which a person is given the choice: if he wishes he may fast, and if he wishes he may break his fast.
